Foreword 

The subject matter of our original volume Wills and Administration in New Jersey has become so changed by reason of new statutes and decisions brought forward during the last two years that it was thought necessary by the author and the publishers to bring forth this second volume. Particularly, it was thought desirable to add the new Revised Statutes Citations. References in each note have been keyed to the Revised Statutes 1937. For example, where P L 1917 p 71 is cited in a note, the supplemental note will read R. S. 2:15-1 (P L 1917 p 71). Cases in this supplementary book have been brought down to August 1939. 

The publishers would like to express their gratitude to the author, Mr. Clapp, for the thorough scholarly nature of this book. 

The reader will find that this supplementary volume has the sections in the same order as the original book so that by consulting the index of the original book it will be possible to work out a legal question right up to the present date. The table of cases and the table of statutes in this volume will be found very useful to the reader where he knows the particular statute or case but wishes to find its application.

The forms in this book should be very practical to the reader inasmuch as those printed in the book are tried and proven. Although the text of our book does not cover Adoption and Guardianship, we have inserted a number of forms on these two subjects which should prove very useful to the reader inasmuch as there is a great deal of confusion at the present time on these subjects. 

The publishers and Mr. Clapp would like to express their appreciation to the Hon. Charles A. Otto who has supplied the forms for this book. Also, we would like to express our thanks to Miss R.

Allin, Special Probate Clerk of Union County, whose valuable assistance has accomplished much in making this a good form book. 

Judges, vice ordinaries, surrogates and their assistants should find this work practical in settling puzzling problems. 

This book, as indicated by the pocket, will be kept up to date by future supplements, whenever necessary.

October, 1939. 

THE PUBLISHERS.

$3. When Gifts of Chattels Violate the Wills Act. 

(1) In general as to the law of gifts inter vivos in New Jersey, see 61 NJ LJ 141. In the case of a tangible chose, delivery of the voucher transmits the title to the chose (Cook v. Lum 55 L 373, 26 A 803). R.S. 14:8-27 

(P L 1916 c 191 §1). R.S. 14:8-35 (P L 1916 c 191, §9). R.S. 7:2-49 (C S 3740 

§49). A delivery to one of two joint tenants or to one of two tenants in common is a delivery to both. East Rutherford B. & L. Ass'n v. Rutherford 87 E 

375, 100 A 931 E & A; but see §8 at n 12.

A gift of any chattel, except an intangible chose, is governed by the law of the place where the chattel is. Restatement of Law of Conflicts, §257, 261; but see Cutts v. Najdrowski 123 E 481, 198 A 885 E & A as to money. It is a matter of dispute as to what law governs the assignment of an intangible chose. §348 Restatement above holds the law of the place of contracting governs; but see 58 N J L J 225. (2) Peppler v. Roffe 122 E 570, 194 A 548 E & A; In re Fulper 93 E 

112, 115 A 661. There may be a donative intent even though the donor continues until his death to receive the income from the subject of the gift. First 

National Bank of Perth Amboy v. Keller 122 E 481, 194 A 554 E & A. 

(3) §133 at n 4. 

(4) Hackensack Trust Co. v. Nowacki 124 E 565, 3 A2d 615. Certifi cates of stock were indorsed in blank and kept in alleged donee's safe deposit box but alleged donor had access to the box and treated the stock as his own. 

Held no valid gift inter vivos. In re Brueck 122 E 329, 194 A 60, mod. 124 1

Gifts Causa Mortis. 

$4. (6) If the donor recovers or survives the peril which existed at the time of the gift, the gift is revoked. A condition that the gift is effective only if he does not survive that peril is to be implied; but if expressed, it supports the argument that the gift was causa mortis. Jadzevicz v. Adams 122 E 6, 191 A 803 E & A. 85. 

When a Trust Violates the Wills Act. 

(1) See §1 n 8. A gift of property to A in trust to be distributed after settlor's death to B and C is testamentary where the legal title does not pass to A in settlor's life. In re Brueck 122 E 329, 194 A 60, aff'd in this respect 

124 E 62, 199 A 61 E & A. (3) A cannot make a trust without executing it as a will, in the case where the trust needs to be supplemented by a will to put it into effect. E.g. a gift in trust to trustees named in settlor's ambulatory will where no property was to be delivered until the settlor's death. 

However, one may give his property by will to the trustees or beneficiaries, or for the purposes, stated in a presently effective trust made before or after the will, provided the trust has a non-testamentary design. E.g. "to the beneficiary of the trust dated-". §38.

To be distinguished from these situations is a recent case where in a presently effective trust the settlor conferred on herself the income for life and a power of appointment by will. Prior to making a trust she had made a will which did not purport to exercise all powers testatrix might have at her death, but merely gave property to B, C, etc. The court held that the settlor meant to provide for the legatees of her will if it remained unrevoked at her death. Fidelity Union Trust Co. v. Hall 125 E 419, 6 A2d 124.

The court said that she incorporated into the trust her will which was ambulatory, but the case may be regarded rather as one of an inter vivos trust with the income to the settlor for life and the remainder to form part of her estate at her death provided she died testate and if she died intestate, to her heirs. The trust was not testamentary. Equally as good is the case where the settlor reserves a power of appointment (and not as the settlor did in the case cited, "testamentary proprietorship" §195). Would not the trust also be non-testamentary if there were no life estate to the settlor but merely a duty in the trustees to accumulate income during the settlor's life and to pay the same and the corpus under a testamentary power conferred on the settlor with a gift in default; the power acting as a springing executory use? (5) Distinguish this case from an inter vivos trust of personalty to T in trust for A during settlor's life with remainder to settlor's executor on his death. The latter case is good, for if the settlor had not created the remainder, the remainder interest would have passed as a reversion (§145) to the settlor's executor under his will. Pedrick v. Guarantee Trust Co. 123 E 395, 197 A 909. In the case of the text there is no effective passage of title to trustees during settlor's life; in the Pedrick case there is, with a gift over of personalty to the person who would take the legal title to all settlor's personalty. 43 Harvard Law Review at p 535. §8 n 8. But when the property 2

TESTAMENTARY DISPOSITION WILLS ACT 

is realty, the gift over on settlor's death to an executor not named trustee in the will would be void. (6) Sears v. Grover 116 E 111, 172 A 525 E & A may be distinguished (see 116 E 118) on the ground that the court was not carrying out the trust but imposing a constructive trust in favor of the estate of the settlor to prevent unjust enrichment by the trustee. The result is no different in that case whether the express trust was carried out or the parties were restored as nearly as possible to the status quo ante. Cf. §12 at n 9. (7) Cf. Fidelity Union Trust Co. v. Hall 125 E 419, 6 A2d 124. (8) The validity of a trust of any chattel except an intangible chose in action (§3 n 1) is determined by the law of the situs of the chattel. Restatement of Conflict of Laws $294; see Cutts v. Najdrowski 123 E 481, 198

A 885 E & A. The validity of a trust of an intangible chose in action created by a transaction inter vivos is determined by the law of the place where the transaction takes place. Cutts v. Najdrowski supra; cf. In re Lucas 124 E 

347, 1 A2d 929 (construing New York Law apparently. The rule §6 at n 3 

does not apply in New York). Contra 2 Beale Conflicts of Laws 1018. R.S. 

25:1-3 (CS 2611 §3). R.S. 25:1-4 (C S 2611 §4). 

(9) A power of revocation can be exercised only by the persons to whom it it reserved and in the manner specified in the reservation. Lippincott v. Williams 63 E 130, 51 A 467; Clark v. Freeman 121 E 35, 188 A 493.

. Bank Accounts. 

(6) Strike out the second, fourth and fifth paragraphs of the note and insert: If an account is payable to A and B, a joint tenancy is created. §157. 

Where A and B are husband and wife a tenancy in common is created. §157. 

See §92 for the substitution of the word "and" for the word "or".

In a joint tenancy, either joint owner (or a creditor attaching, or levying on, the interest of a joint owner) may sever the unity of interest without the consent of the other and in the event of severance the joint owners became tenants in common entitled to equal shares and the right of survivorship terminates. Dover Trust Co. v. Brooks 111 E 40, 160 A 890. Cf. Matter of Sutter 258 N Y 104, 176 N E 310; Moskowitz v. Marrow 251 N Y 380, 167 N E 506. When the proceeds of an account held by persons as joint tenants are invested in property the title to which is taken in both names with words of survivorship, it is said that there is a presumption that the property is held by the parties as joint tenants with survivorship. Hahn v. Ironbound Trust Co. 94 E 123, 118 A 744; but cf. Reeves v. Reeves 102 E 436, 141 A 175; cf. Crane v. Brewer 73 E 558, 68 A 78.

If an account is payable to A and B or either of them with a right of survivorship, it may be said that a joint tenancy has been created with a power in each tenant to withdraw the whole. Dover Trust Co. v. Brooks su pra. Cf. New Jersey Title Guarantee & Trust Co. v. Archibald 91 E 82, 108 

A 434 E & A. For the statute, see at n 10. (9) Under the authorities in §157, even though the deposit by two persons constitutes the proceeds of a business carried on by them, the deposit may be held to be a joint tenancy.

102 E 436, 141 A 175. (18) See §6 supra, paragraph numbered 6. Cf. §34 n 9 inter vivos transactions. (19) Strike out from the text the words "The effect of".

§11. Contracts to Devise and Bequeath..... The construction of the contract will present problems. Where there is an agreement by A to make a devise to B on B's making certain payments and where B predeceases A, the property will go to the devisee of B on payments being made, for it will be construed an agreement to devise to B "or to those to whom B shall direct" (19). (1) See §1 n 15. Many of the cases cited in this section are cases where there is a contract made by one to give property at his death without having a thought in his mind of making a will. Such a contract is regarded by the courts as though it were exactly the same as a contract to make a will and is valid. §1 n 12. 

(2) The law courts have no power to create exceptions to the Wills 

Act on the ground of fraud. §12.

$12. When Equity Will Disregard the Wills Act. For equit able aider of a testamentary power of appointment defectively executed, see §202. (5) $459. (9) Sick v. Weigand 123 E 239, 197 A 413 E & A (A promised B that if B devised and bequeathed property to A, A would devise and bequeath to C so much of the property as remained unexpended at A's death, except that which might be needed for carrying charges and medical expenses. After B's death, A conveyed the property to X. A died. Decreed that X hold it in trust for C). Where B's will shows that property is given to A on trust without defining the trust, and then after B's death, A has promised to hold the property in trust for C, an individual or a charity which was the beneficiary the testator intended to benefit, C will not take but the property will be held under a resulting trust for B's estate. Smith v. Smith 54 E 1, 32 A 1069, aff'd on op. 55 E 821, 41 A 1116. See Restatement Law of Trusts §55 (g), 37 Har vard Law Review 527; cf. Ramsdell v. Streeter 62 E 718, 48 A 575. (12) If A knew dehors the will that he was to hold it on trust, but the beneficiary of the trust is never designated, then A holds the property in trust for B's estate. See n 9 supra. §38. Disposition under a Will by Reference to a Non- Testamentary Act. 

(1) For the doctrine of this section, cf. in general §5 n 3. Condit v. 

DeHart 62 L 78, 40 A 776 is not supportable under §38 as a disposition by reference to an act of independent significance but only under §37 as a case of incorporation by reference. There was no independent significance in the clause of the son's will "I appoint to my wife the property over which my

MAKING AND REVOCATION OF A WILL 

father has given me a power." Had the father's gift been to the son's legatees (and not to such persons as the son may appoint), and had the son's will given to his wife all his own property, the son's disposition would have had independent significance for the disposition would have passed the property that was fully his own as well as that of his father over which he had only testamentary proprietorship. Cf. §471 (e) Tentative Restatement of 

Property. 

(3) Erratum: Insert the word "such" after the word "since" in the fourth line of the note. The testator in making an advance intends both (a) to help the beneficiary now and (b) to reduce the bequest. Clause (a) states his primary intent. It is sometimes said that the detriment incurred by the testator in making the gift takes the case out of the statute. See 

Hill v. Bloom 41 E 276, 7 A 438 (will provided that benefactions were to be offset by obligations or evidences of debt; held that receipts for gifts to beneficiaries were evidences causing a set off). For other cases, see §103; §411; cf. 60 N J L J 102, 103 (the author errs in considering that a present gift is testamentary. A present gift has clearly a non-testamentary significance). If an advancement constitutes a debt or legal obligation running from the beneficiary to the testator, there is no difficulty in offsetting it. 

Youmans v. Youmans 26 E 149. Where a beneficiary obtained from the testator in the testator's lifetime some property and then gave him a receipt “in full, in lieu of dowry," it was held beneficiary contracted not to claim any share to which he was entitled by intestacy. Havens v. Thompson 

26 E 383. See especially §411 n 6.

§42. Revocation by Another Will. In General. 

(1) In re Block 15 M 233, 190 A 315 (the court talks of "deeds of revocation" delivered "in escrow" as though one could have a written revocation not executed as a will). A nuncupative will cannot revoke a written

-  

(3) In an irreconcilable conflict, the later will or codicil controls. §247 

Tent. Restatement of Property. Where there are two wills wholly inconsistent, the second is to be probated even though its practical effect is purely revocatory, the beneficiary under the second will having predeceased the testator. In re Drake 15 M 484, 192 A 428. Cf. §43 infra. The question as to whether the beneficiaries are alive is one for the court of construction, the Chancery, not the Probate, Court. However, In re Drake presents no case for the doctrine of dependent relative revocation because neither the condition nor the mistake appears on the face of the second will. There should be no presumption that where the revocation is effected by an inconsistent will without a revocatory clause, the testator would prefer to have the prior will to no will. But see §247 Tentative Restatement of Property.

Smith 123 E 602, 199 A 35 E & A. (17) See $39. $47. Revival. (2) In re Block 15 M 233, 190 A 315 (Orp. Ct.), argues that a revocatory clause in a will is testamentary, that it could not have been intended to take effect at a different time than the rest of the will. The contrary argument is supported by most authority, as conceded by In re Block, and is 10

MAKING AND REVOCATION OF A WILL 

thought to be stronger. It is a strange person who believes that all his inconsistent wills exist side by side until the moment of his death, the earlier ones intended to be kept in existence in case he wishes to abolish the later ones, his reason being to save paper in rewriting the earlier wills. This argument applies not only to a revocatory clause in a will, but to the case of an inconsistent will without an express clause of revocation; and would lead to the abolition of the doctrine of revival, except in the case where the testator makes a subsequent will and states therein that a prior will is to take effect if the subsequent will is revoked. 

It is indefensible to get one result when the will is revoked by act to the document (as by the cancellation of the will) or by a revocatory instrument executed as a will but having no testamentary clauses, and a different one when it is revoked by a later will or by a clause in a later will; one cannot say in the first and second cases that the revocation is presumably effective presently and in the third and fourth cases that it is presumably effective on testator's death. A testator's intent is not different in one case than in another. It should be the rule that all revocations are intended to take effect presently except when a testator otherwise provides in a subsequent will. Such a rule would put the doctrine of revival on a sound basis. 

The doctrine of revival is a piece of fine-spun legal reasoning reaching a rule contrary to a layman's expectations; such rules weaken the law. Few testators would think that by revoking one will, an earlier will comes to life. The only pragmatic justification for the doctrine is that it saves paper in rewriting the earlier will. 3 Newark University Law Review 113. (4) Erratum: A paragraph commences with the words in the text: "Even if." Even if the doctrine of revival is to be retained in the case of a revocatory clause or in the case of an inconsistent will without a revocatory clause, the automatic revivor rule should not be tolerated. The simplicity, extolled in In re Block 15 M 233, 190 A 315, of the automatic revivor rule, must give way to the hardship it occasions in defeating the testator's intention. The logic to the automatic revivor rule is not "unassailable" as represented by the Block Case; a testator not only can, but usually does, intend to have his revocatory clause take effect presently even though the remainder of the will is ineffective until his death. See n 2. (7) Erratum: Add note figure 7 after last word in section. The omitted note reads: The cases of revival all arise in the probate court. A 199, aff'g 84 E 135, 92 A 952; Randall v. Beatty 31 E 643; In re Block 

15 M 233, 190 A 315; but cf. McGill v. Trust Co. of New Jersey §47 n 6.

Like the matter of revocation (§39) it is a matter for that court alone. 11

Where a testator intends by certain words one thing but the dictionary says they mean another thing, there may be said to be an ambiguity. 

But in such an ambiguity direct statements of intention are not admissible. 

The ambiguity is not objective as to the testator; and, therefore, the court has not the same assurance that there is an ambiguity that it has in the case of the ambiguity noted in the text. If the proof of the testator's thoughts in that regard are admissible at all (and that is the subject of §53) that proof must come from the testator's "oblique" and not his direct statements.

Martin 124 E 106, 199 A 589 E & A, the court setting no standard holds that the life tenants were excluded from the remainder to "heirs and next of kin" where they were the sole heirs and next of kin. The case leaves the matter open unless it be that in an elaborate will, the scheme of the will is so disrupted by giving the remainder to A that the courts will exclude A from taking the remainder. The thought is that the testator could not be deemed to have elaborately provided for income to A when he intended A to have his whole estate. But the case will not fit with the rest of the law.

(1) By "survivor" is meant one who lives either (a) after another has died or (b) after an event has happened. Freeman 121 E 35, 188 A 493 (in that case the first person had not disabled himself from acting). $78. "My Estate," "Property." . . . . A gift of personal property to A for life and on his death to his estate does not give A a testamentary power of appointment but the property passes on his death to his executor or administrator (10). $93. A Gift "to A, in case of his Death (simpliciter or on a contingency)" to B. (3) See $150. (4) The rule applies in the case of a gift to A simpliciter, as well as to where there is a gift to A and his heirs or A absolutely or the like. See 

Steinhart v. Wolf 95 E 132, 122 A 886; New Jersey Title etc. Co. v. Snyder 

103 E 502, 143 A 725; Beatty's Adm'r v. Montgomery's Ex'rs 21 E 324. But if the limitation is to A for life and "in case of his death" to B, the quoted phrase refers to A's death at any time before and after the testator's death. 

2 Jarman (6th Am. ed) * 1571. On the other hand it was held that where there was a gift to A and B and their issue (creating under the fee tail statute then in force, a life estate in A and B with a remainder to their children) and in case of the death of A and B then to C, it was held that "in case of death" refers to the death of A and B before the testator's death. 

Content v. Dalton 121 E 391, 396, 190 A 328, aff'd 122 E 425, 194 A 286.

The rule applies even though B does not take an unconditional fee. §263 

comment (d) Tentative Restatement of Property. 

(5) $264 Tentative Restatement of Property. 

(6) That is, B dies unsurvived by issue at the time of his death (§94) whenever that may occur. Accord §267 and p 190 Tentative Restatement of 

Property. However, where the gift is to A and his heirs or absolutely to be held in trust until A attains 21 and if A dies without issue, then to B, the rule is that B takes unless A dies without issue before 21. The gift over to 

C might take place if A died without issue either (1) before the testator, (2) before attaining 21 or (3) whenever B dies. Alternative (2) is taken instead of alternative (3). §268 Tentative Restatement of Property; Wurts' Ex'rs v Page 19 E 365. 

(8) Sed $208 at n 13 et seq. A gift over in case A dies without issue means in case A dies without issue surviving him. §93. The question is whether B takes on A's death where A dies without issue after X. §269 

Tentative Restatement of Property soundly lays down a presumption that B takes only where the event of A's death without issue surviving occurs before X's interest terminates. It is apparent from §93 n 8 that the weight of authority in New Jersey can be counted as in accord with the Law Institute. Erratum: In abstracting the facts of Hampton v. Byrne 123 E 6, 14, 195 A 848, aff'd 124 E 273, 1 A2d 464 (but not if there is a provision for the distribution of the estate at an earlier time, see 123 E 20). (3) It has been held that periodic payments charged on the corpus and income of a special trust fund are an annuity. Bridgeport v. City Trust Co. 

32 B T A 1181, aff'd 84 Fed (2d) 991. If the principal of the fund should abate, the residue presumably would remain liable as for the payment of any general legacy. (§107. Distinguish the gift of the income of a fund, §109 n 17.) 

On the other hand it could be provided in the will that after the setting apart of the trust fund, the residue is to be relieved of liability for the payment of the annuity in case the trust fund is exhausted. (4) First Nat'l Bank v. Levy 123 E 21, 25, 195 A 820; Byrne v. Byrne 

123 E 6, 14, 195 A 848, aff'd 124 E 273, 1 A2d 464 (an annuity payable out of a designated fund is a demonstrative legacy). If an annuity is payable only out of the income of a fund and the income is deficient in one year, the deficiency must be made up out of a surplus in other years. First Nat'l Bank of Toms River v. Levy 123 E 21, 31, 195 A 820. But it cannot be made up out of other funds. First Nat'l Bank of Toms River v. Levy supra; Hopkins v. Remy 64 E 12, 53 A 676; Kling v. Van Cleeve 106 E 302, 150 A 684. §420 n 1. (6) But see In re Hardy's Estate 86 E 405, 99 A 326 E & A.

In Lib erty Title & Trust Co. v. Stevens 115 E 506, 171 A 531, aff'd on op. 117 E 

404, 176 A 167 annuities were permitted to be purchased from insurance companies with the consent of annuitants. The testator may not wish a fund to be set apart as where the annuity is charged on realty. Wyckoff v. Wyckoff 

48 E 113, 119, 21 A 287, aff'd on op. 49 E 344, 25 A 963. §109 n 10; §112 n 15; 

§420 n 1; §421 at n 9; §417 at n 7. 

(8) See n 3 supra. The use by the testator of the word "annuity" will not make the gift an annuity as distinguished from a gift out of the income of a fund, except in a rare case or in the case noted in the text where the testator has without more directed the executor to set up a fund to produce an annuity to pay, say A, $1,000.00 a year. (9) That the residue of the estate abates before the corpus of the fund provided to pay annuities, see Merritt v. Merritt 48 E 1, 9, 10, 11, 21 A 128, aff'd on op. 51 E 638, 30 A 429. Where annuities are bequeathed to certain persons and a fund provided for their payment with a gift over of the fund on the death of such persons, the courts incline to hold that such persons only look to the income of the fund. But if there is a gift over of the residue of the fund, then they may resort to the corpus if necessary. Byrne v. Byrne 123 

E 6, 195 A 848, aff'd 124 E 273, 1 A2d 464. (11) 

Howell v. Cornell 31 L 374.

§104. Abatement. In General. 

(1) In this and other connections the words "legacies" and "devises" are sometimes used to refer to the property bequeathed and devised. See §82 n 2. (5) First Nat'l Bank of Toms River v. Levy 123 E 21, 30, 195 A 820. $105. Legacies and Devises on a Consideration and in lieu of 

Dower or Curtesy. (1) It is believed that despite this rule, A, a residuary legatee who accepts the legacy in satisfaction of a debt, may not claim preference to a legatee taking under an earlier clause in the will, unless the preference is expressly stated. By electing to take the residue, A necessarily took what might be left of the estate after excluding debts and expenses and the foregoing legacies and devises; for that is what is meant by a residuary gift. 

For the definition of the word "residue", see §78 n 7. (4) Cf. Landis v. Cumberland Trust Co. 92 E 689, 116 A 686 E & A. (5) A devise in lieu of dower charged with legacies is subject to the legacies; the devise is not superior to the legacies. Stephenson v. Brown 4 E

§106. Order in which Assets are applied for Payment of Debts. 

(1) 

See §330 at n 10. (2) §327. However personal property undisposed of must be applied to decedent's debts before property bequeathed and devised. Dodson v. Sevars 

53 E 347, 33 A 388 E & A. (3) A direction in a will to pay out of the residue the Federal Estate tax against "gifts herein made" does not render the residuary estate liable for the Federal Estate tax due as to property passing under a general power exercised by the will where the testator did not regard the exercise of the power as a "gift herein made". Fidelity Union Trust Co. v. Suydam 125 E 458. (10) Hattersley v. Bissett 52 E 693, 30 A 86; contra Langstroth v. 

Golding 41 E 49, 3 A 151. (14) See n 17; §330. Holding blended residuary realty and personalty to abate pro rata. See Federal Trust Co. v. Ost 120 E 475, 186 A 579. Holding that residuary realty abates before specific legacies and devises, see Langstroth v. Golding supra (it is not clear whether the realty was charged for debts. §330 at n 5 and 6). That charged realty abates ahead of specific legatees, see n 21. When it is said (Whitehead v. Gibbons supra this note, and Morris v. Higbie 27 A 438; In re Banks (1905) 1 Ch 547; 2 Jarman Wills (6th ed) *1461) that in order to have charged realty applied before personalty, the personalty must be exhausted the question is, as will be seen from the cases, one between charged realty and personalty given in the residuary clause and has nothing to do with personalty otherwise given. (15) See §330 at n 9; §327 at n 1. (16) It seems clear that residuary legatees cannot call on general legatees for contribution. Shreve v. Shreve 17 E 487, 492 E & A; Thomas v. Thomas 17 E 356. (17) Residuary real estate is usually charged with debts either because of a direction that debts be paid or because the residuary realty is blended with residuary personal estate. §330. Hence in the usual case residuary real estate abates before pecuniary and general bequests given in clauses prior to the residuary clause. N 15; Theobold on Wills 7th ed 828. (18) Many of the authorities in this section place specific devises in this relative position among benefactions. Since specific legacies abate with specific devises ratably, it is clear that specific legacies stand in the same relative position. (20) See $198. §111. Payment of Charges. 

(3) 

Cf. §417. (7) In the case of a general charge for debts or a general charge for debts and legacies, the only way for the purchaser to obtain a discharge therefrom with any convenience would be to empower the executor to give the discharge. There may be many debts and it would be difficult to ascertain who the debtors are. There is nothing in the statute at §332 giving the executor the power to grant a discharge where testator's lands are purchased within a year of testator's death; but that direction may be implied. The statute was in force (as §26 of R.S. 1846 p 660) prior to Dewey's Ex'rs v. 

Ruggles 25 E 35 where the agreement to sell was made by an executor within a year after testatrix' death. The will directed the debts to be paid creating a charge on her lands, §330; and a power of sale was implied by virtue of this charge. §269. It was held that the executor's deed discharged the lien created by the will and the purchaser need not see to the application of the purchase money. No mention, however, was made of the statute. Cf. $113. Lapse. (1) A gift may be made to the estate of a deceased person; but there is a strong presumption that a testator would not want an estate to take. 

Griffin v. Chodynicki 123 E 253, 197 A 4 E & A; Brown v. Turpan 122 E 

305, 194 A 63. See §209 n 5. (4) A gift to A and B jointly prevents lapse if one predeceases the testator, as the whole will go to the survivor; but if the gift is to them as tenants in common and A predeceases the testator, his share will lapse. Damron v. Mast 121 E 489, 191 A 467. Cf. §157; §114. Where there is a gift to

A his "heirs, executors, administrators and assigns," the quoted words do not prevent a lapse. They are words of limitation and not of substitution. 

King v. King 125 E 94, 4 A2d 405 E & A. See Langstroth v. Golding §70 n 6. 

(5) But see Izan v. Butter 2 Price 34, 146 Eng. Rep. 13; Martland v. 

Adair 3 Ves 231, 130 Eng. Rep. 984; Sibthorp v. Maxon 3 Atk 580, 26 Eng. 

Rep. 1134; Elliot v. Davenport 1 P Wms. 83, 24 Eng. Rep. 304; Toplis v. 

Baker 1 Cox's P. W. p 16 5th ed notes. 

(6) R.S. 3:2-18 (C S 5866 §22). As to the application to an appoint ment under a power, see §200 at n 12. (7) As to an adopted child, see Smallwood v. Smallwood 121 E 126, 186 A 775. (10) The statute does not apply to a case where the gift was intended to be made personally to a brother. Griffin v. Chodynicki 123 E 253, 197 A 4 

E & A ("to my brother if he can be located within one year after my death").

(5) If there is no child living at testator's death, the limitation is construed to the parent for life with a remainder to his children. So where the gift is to "A, B and C and their respective children" and one of them is childless on the testator's death, A, B and C all take life estates with remainders to their children respectively. §283 Tentative Restatement of Property.

§117. Gifts to Creditors. 

(1) Sears v. Grover 116 E 111, 172 A 525 E & A. $118. (3) 

Gifts to Debtors and Release of Debts. 

First Nat'l Bank, South River v. Hurley 122 E 53, 191 A 858 E & A (but where the testatrix extends the time for the payment of the debt, the legacy must be paid promptly and the payment cannot be deferred until the time for the payment of the debt). The setoff must be effected even though the debt is secured by a mortgage and although the legatee is the executor and is solvent. In re Kraeuter 125 E 120, 4 A2d 383; cf. §313 at n 

8 et seq. See §429 n 15. (9) A will will rarely operate technically as a release even supposing it to be sealed, for any delivery of the will made by the releasor would have to be made during his life and would be presumably intended to be ambulatory and therefore ineffective. Elliot v. Davenport 1 P Wms 83, 24 Eng. Rep. 

304. A release by will therefore generally is given effect as a legacy and hence the amount released constitutes assets liable for the payment of testator's debts. Rider v. Wager 2 P Wms 328, 333, 24 Eng. Rep. 751, 753. (10) The theory of Liddell's Ex'rs v. Starr was overruled in Irwin v. 

Johnson §118 n 11; but the holding of the case is nevertheless sound. (15) §95 at n 19. 

§120. Election. In General. . . . . Where one bound to elect has taken possession of property given to him under a will, a prima facie case arises to the effect that he elected to accept thereunder. 

He may show that he had not fairly and understandingly made his election or that he had made it under a mistake of law or fact (8).

§136A. Proof of Beneficiary's Survivorship. There is neither a presumption of simultaneous death (1) nor a presumption of survivorship in a common disaster by reason of age or sex, the question being one of fact the burden of proof of which lies on the claimant usually. The burden does not require absolute and direct proof, but only such proof circumstantial or otherwise as would 32

convince an ordinary reasonable individual (2). The conclusion reached must be the more probable hypothesis with reference to the possibility of other hypotheses, that is, it must exclude any other equally fair and reasonable result (3). If the court cannot determine whether the testator or his sole legatee survived, the testator's estate will go to his next of kin by operation of law (4). (1) Masonic Temple Ass'n v. Hannum 120 E 183, 184 A 414; Union 

Life Ins. Co. v. Elizabeth Trust Co. 119 E 505, 183 A 181; cf. §95 at n 5 and 6. (2) Hackensack Trust Co. v. Hackensack Hosp. Ass'n 120 E 14, 183 A 

723; In re Le Van 123 E 463, 198 A 278; cases n 1. (3) In re Le Van supra n 1. Cf. the established rule of circumstantial evidence stated in another connection. Austin v. Penn R. R. Co. 82 L 416, 81 A 739 E & A. (4) In re Le Van supra n 2.

§139A. The Effect of Stating a Purpose of a Legacy (1). 

It is said (2) that where the purpose of an immediate gift to A is solely to benefit A, the gift is absolute, the statement of purpose being nugatory (3). It is further said that where the purpose of an immediate gift is to benefit other persons as well as the primary donee either (a) a trust may be created entitling all beneficiaries to an equitable estate (4); or (b) it may be left to the discretion of the primary donee as to the quantum of benefit to be conferred on the other donees with no liability on him to account for what is not applied for their benefit (5); or (c) the expression of motive may be merely nugatory and not operate to abridge a previous absolute gift (6).

Where a purpose appears from the face of the will as an obligatory part of the gift and that purpose fails, the gift fails (7). (1) §95 at n 8. Cf. §207 n 4 as to conditions involving some behavior or act on donee's part. 

(2) 1 Jarman Wills (6th ed) *367.

§145. Remainders, Reversions and Reverters. . . Where there are no executory interests, the ultimate estate where created and all estates succeeding the first of the particular estates, are remainders (1). If the ultimate estate is not created by the testator, the law raises it and denominates it a reversion (2). For the clarification of these definitions (though lacking precise authority) it may be said that where a particular estate is created by a will and the ultimate interest passes in the residuary clause, the interest is a remainder; but if it passes under the intestacy statute, the interest is a reversion. (2) For an illustration of an equitable reversion, see Pedrick v. Guarantee Trust Co. 123 E 395, 197 A 909. (3) Byrne v. Byrne 123 E 6, 195 A 848, aff'd 124 E 273, 1 A2d 464. (4) Byrne v. Byrne supra n 3. (5) Byrne v. Byrne supra n 3. 3365

$169. Depleting Assets and Bonds Bought at a Premium. 

Strike from the note §146 and insert §143. 

(1) $170. Increase in Value of Corpus. (6) As to what constitutes the amount of arrears due the life tenant, see §177 particularly n 2. The rule applies in the case of a default in the mortgage when it becomes the trustee's duty to convert the mortgage investment (and the mortgaged property if taken over) into a proper investment. 

The rule applies to any property which the trustee has a duty to convert. 

This duty to convert arises when the property (e.g. the mortgage or the mortgaged property taken over) produces no income or an income substantially less than the current rate of return on trust investments. §240 and §241 Restatement of Trusts. The life tenant should not lose his right to income while the trustee holds the property awaiting a market. He has an investment in the property to the extent of his lost income; he and the taker of the corpus as co-owners have a right to the proceeds in the proportions stated in the text and in that proportion will share the profits or, depending on the amount of the proceeds, the loss. §177 particularly n 2. Likewise the taker of corpus, now a co-owner of the property with the life beneficiary, will be entitled to his fraction of any income received after default. If any income received on the property after default has been paid to the life beneficiary, the amount so paid will be deducted from the amount he would otherwise receive on the sale had the income accumulated and been added to the proceeds of sale. §241 Restatement of Trusts. As to charges and expenses on the property, see §171 n 5. 

§171. Apportionment of Expenses and Losses. In General. 

(1a) In a gift of net income "after deducting all proper charges,” the quoted words are superfluous; they exclude those charges which are ordinarily charged against corpus. Equitable Trust Co. v. Swoboda 113 E 399, 167 A 525. (3) Cf. n 5. (5) Hudson County National Bank v. Woodruff 122 E 444, 194 A 266, mod. 123 E 585, 199 A 399 holds that where there are several mortgages in trust and the trustee takes over the properties, each property is to be regarded as a separate unit and the income from one unit cannot be used to meet the carrying charge of another. The reason these unproductive properties are treated as separate units (as distinguished from the other cases of this note) is that here the properties became unproductive after the making of the trust. Accord Nirdlinger's Estate 331 Pa 135, 200 A 656; Matter of 

Chapal 269 N Y 464, 199 N E 762.

Owning the proportions of the property referred to in §170 n 6 and §177 n 2, the life taker and the remainderman should share in those proportions as co-owners all charges whether they would otherwise have been capital or income charges. The rule applies only to property held in trust which the trustee has a duty to convert as noted in §170 n 6. The charges will include costs of foreclosure (§176), taxes accrued on property prior to taking title (§172 n 8), insurance, current taxes (§172 n 8), current repairs and improvements to "corpus" (§173 n 8) and expenses of selling 39

-  

the property (§176). Where the property is unproductive, all such charges will fall in the first instance on corpus, then corpus will be reimbursed out of the proceeds of liquidation and the net proceeds will be divided in the proportion stated in §170 and $177. Equitable Trust Co. v. Swoboda 113 E 

399, 167 A 525. In the accounting the taker of the corpus is charged with all such charges as of the date of the advance. Then the life taker is debited with his portion of the charges as of the date of the liquidation and then as of that date the taker of the corpus receives a credit for the amount charged income. Likewise if any of such charges have been paid in the first instance out of income, income is first reimbursed from the net proceeds before they are pro rated (§241 comment (d) Restatement of Trusts). The cases do not state it, but whichever makes the advance corpus or income, should also be allowed interest at 4% on the advance from the time of the advance until the time of reimbursement. Two more observations may be made: By paying such charges out of the proceeds of liquidation, the charges are in effect pro rated in the same proportion as the proceeds. The life taker not only pays his portion of the charges but he loses interest on the portion charged against corpus, but this seems equitable. (8) When testator gives his wife a life estate during widowhood "in addition to dower," the rights of dower and quarantine merge in the life estate, leaving the life tenant liable for taxes. Asmus v. Asmus 122 E 485, 194 A 884. $172. Taxes and Assessments. 

(2) Fidelity Union Trust Co. v. Murphy 124 E 201, 1 A2d 201. 

(3) R.S. 54:4-23 (P L 1927 p 576). See §263 at n 7. 

(8) It is held that where a mortgage is put in trust to A for life and the mortgaged property is taken over by the trustee and is unproductive, the taxes and other carrying charges advanced by the trustee for the protection of the mortgaged property are to be charged against corpus temporarily; and ultimately on the sale of the property, they are to be deducted from the gross proceeds realized and thereby apportioned between principal and in come. Fidelity Union Trust Co. v. Murphy 124 E 201, 1 A2d 201 (saying that corpus should be reimbursed for the taxes paid. This is true if it is to be reimbursed out of the gross proceeds of the sale and not wholly out of the proportion of the proceeds that goes to income. §176 n 4; §171 n 5; 

Equitable Trust Co. v. Swoboda 113 E 399, 167 A 525). See further in general Equitable Trust Co. v. Swoboda supra; cf. Hudson County National 

Bank v. Woodruff 122 E 444, 194 A 266, aff'd on op. 123 E 585, 199 A 399.

The taxes accruing before the trustee acquires title to the mortgaged premises are likewise chargeable in the first instance to corpus and then deducted from the proceeds when the trustee ultimately sells the property, being there by apportioned. Fidelity Union Trust Co. v. Murphy 124 E 201, 1 A2d 201; 

Hudson County National Bank v. Woodruff supra. In the extraordinary circumstances of one case, the court held that the taxes on a family homestead held in trust paid by the trustee by reason of the falling through of a sale, are chargeable against corpus as an expense of the sale and in no way against income. The homestead is distinguished from an ordinary investment. Fidelity Union Trust Co. v. Murphy supra; §176 at n 5. 40

$173. Repairs and Improvements. 

(2) R.S. 2:79 (C S 5790, 5791). (5) Repairs designed to keep the property intact and in its original operating condition are to be charged against income except when the property is one which is held in trust and the trustee under a duty to convert it. 

§170 n 6; §171 n 5. See §418 n 15. 

(6) When repairs will add to the utility the property had when the life estate commenced, then, with the exception herein noted, they are to be paid (a) by the life tenant when they are made by him (as where he has possession of the property)—see at n 11; or (b) by the trustee out of corpus when the property is held in trust and the improvements are properly made by the trustee for the benefit of both the life taker and the taker of the corpus, the life tenant paying his share by losing the income on the corpus thus appropriated. 73. The exception to the above rules arises where the property is held in trust and the trustee is under a duty to convert it, the life beneficiary and the taker of the corpus each owning a certain percentage of the property and each being chargeable for his percentage of the improvements. §171 n 5.

$176. Administration and Other Expenses. . . . . The cost of foreclosing a mortgage which is limited for life and over is payable out of corpus and remains chargeable against corpus until the 41

――  

mortgaged property is liquidated; then it is deducted from the proceeds of the sale and returned to corpus whether there is a profit or a loss on the sale and the proceeds are then apportioned (4). Commissions paid real estate agents for the sale of property limited for life and over, subject to a power of sale, are paid by corpus and are in no way chargeable against income (5) except in the case of unproductive mortgaged property taken over by a trustee mortagee and analagous situations elsewhere mentioned (6). (21) See at n 38. 

(26) §295 comment (j) Tentative Restatement of Property. 

(34) Suppose B whether first born or otherwise is the first of A's children to reach 21, and suppose B was living at the testator's death or is born thereafter and reaches 21 prior to the life tenant's death. If B is 21 at the testator's death the gift then vests; or if he is not 21, the gift vests as soon as he attains 21. Then at the life tenant's death the class closes and if B is dead, B's representatives take a share and the others in the class then existent take if they reach 21.

If no child living at the testator's death was then 21 or if no child then living or thereafter born attains 21 before the life tenant's death, then the class closes when the first of the class attains 21 and the others in the class then take if they attain 21. §295 comment (1) Restatement of Property. 

$188. Rule against Perpetuities. In General. 

(1) The rule is not one of interpretation, but of property. Central 

Hanover etc. Co. v. Helme 121 E 406, 190 A 53. (2) All future interests are alienable today excepting interests whose takers are either not in being or are not ascertained (§134 and §179); but these interests are good if they do not conflict with the Rule despite their inalienability. Furthermore, except as rendered void by the Rule, a future contingent interest is alienable when the contingency consists of an uncertain event; and, hence, the Rule destroys an otherwise alienable interest. (5) As to powers of sale, see Foley v. Devine 95 E 473, 476, 123 A 

248; Melvin v. Hoffman 290 Mo 464. The Rule was intended as a restriction on the power to transfer property, not on the power to make a contract and consequently has presented difficulties where a contract creates an equitable interest as an option to buy land. §440. If a contract creates an equitable interest at the time of the contract, there is no question of contingent vesting. An option to buy land does not create a vested equitable interest in the land but an equitable interest remaining contingent on an uncertain event, namely the exercise of the option. Therefore, options to purchase land which are in gross have been held within the Rule. London & S. W. Ry. Co. v. Gomm 20 Ch. Div. 5, Darton v. Thaw 246 Pa. 348; but cf. Knightsbridge Estates v. Byrne (1938) 2 All Eng. L. Rep. 444, 452.

Such an option may prevent improvement of the land. However, the authorities are divided as to the validity of an option to buy the fee, where the option is given a lessee in a long term lease. See Worthing Corp. v. Heather 45

- -  (1906) 2 Ch. 532; Eastman Marble Co. v. Vermont Marble Co. 236 Mass. 

138; Hollander v. Central etc. Co. 109 Md. 131; Abbot 27 Yale L. J. 878;

Langeluttig 17 Va. L. Rev. 461. The Rule is held to apply to a stock option where the option is specifically enforceable. Kingston v. Home Life 11 Del. 

Ch. 258, 266.

As to Revocable Trusts, in general, the period of the Rule should run from the time the settlor loses his power to revoke, as he has the dominus of the estate. See 45 Harvard Law Review 896; see Equitable 

Trust Co. v. Pratt 193 N Y Supp. 152. Mortgages are not within the Rule. See

Knightsbridge Estates v. Byrne supra. As to possibilities of reverter, see 

§178 n 3. 

(7) See Strong v. Shatto 45 Cal App 29. (8) Randolph v. Randolph 40 E 73. 

(14) §273 Tentative Restatement of Property holds that a man or woman is presumably capable of having children at any time but for the purposes of distributing an estate or terminating a trust, this presumption is rebuttable. (Also see p 199 Draft No. 9 for authorities). (16) Central Hanover etc. Co. v. Helme 121 E 406, 190 A 53. See for an obsolete exception, §194 n 7. See also §194 n 8. This view is preferable to the one which moulds the gift to suit the Rule, varying the meaning of words to conform to a Rule which, it is fairly obvious, the testator did not know. But see Shepard v. Union etc. Trust Co. 106 Conn 627, 635; 

Mereto's Estate 311 Pa 374, 379; Freund 33 Harvard Law Review 526, 535.

The analogy sometimes made to constitutional law (U. S. v. Del. & H. R. R. 

Co. 213 U S 366, 407, 408) is not exact as it may be assumed that the constitution is known to a legislature.

§195. Powers of Appointments. In General. 

(1) But a power of appointment, like all express powers, will not survive the death of a donee. §276. (2) 

But cf. a power of disposal, n 10. 

(3) For other powers, see §441 Tentative Restatement of Property. 

(4) §268 et seq. 

(7) A power of appointment is a power to designate the transferees of the property or the shares in which it shall be received. §441 Tentative 

Restatement of Property. It is thereby distinguished as stated in the text from a power of disposal less ambiguously called a power of user, such as a power to use principal presently or a power to use by will (testamentary proprietorship), or a power in the taker of a life interest to augment a gift of income to himself out of principal. Two results from this distinction may be noted: (A) A general power to use by deed is the equivalent of a fee when given with an estate to A simpliciter. See §152; §195 n 10 infra. 

But an estate to A with a general power to appoint by deed does not con stitute a fee (§441 comment (b) Tentative Restatement of Property), for example, it cannot be subjected to the claims of creditors unless the power is exercised. §198 infra. (B) In the case of a power of appointment, the appointee takes title from the donor of the power (§441 comment (b) Tentative Restatement of Property). In the case of a power of user, he takes title from the donee of the power. (8) The cases cited in this note are cases where A has a testamentary power of disposition with a gift over in case of intestacy. (9) Likewise as to a gift to A for life, then to his estate. See §78 n 10. (10) See n 7 and n 8 supra; §150 n 4; §212 n 11; §78 n 10. Cf.

Winterbaum v. Duesel §154 n 5. Holding contrary to Kent v. Armstrong 6 

E 637, 639, and saying that a life estate with an absolute power to dispose of the remainder by will probably constitutes a fee, see Condit v. Reynolds 

66 L 242, 244, 49 A 540 E & A; §152 n 10; §192 at n 11 et seq; §198 n 1.

A life estate with a gift over on intestacy confers a power of appointment.

$198. Appointed Property as Assets. 

(1) Where A has a life estate and a general power to appoint at his death and he exercises the power, the appointed property becomes assets for creditors. See cases §198 n 1; also Johnson v. Cushing 15 N.H. 298, 41 Am.

Dec. 694, 706. A could have appointed to his creditors; and hence must be just before he is generous. Cf. §195 n 14; n 15. For the purposes of the rule of the text a power is exercised only to the extent that the exercise is valid. Whether a power is exercised for a volunteer or a creditor, the property is subjected to creditors. In general, see §449-456 Tentative Restatement of Property. The appointed property is chargeable with administration expenses and taxes of donee's estate. §452 comment (f) and §451 comment (f) Tentative Restatement of Property; O'Donnell v. Barbey 129 Mass 

453; contra Seward v. Kaufman 119 E 44, 50, 180 A 857. But not with dower or curtesy, §455 Tentative Restatement of Property. 

(3) The weight of authority holds that it is a question of convenience and economy of administration in each case as to whether the property should be distributed by the executor (or trustee) of the donor or the executor of the donee. Under this rule ordinarily if both the donor's domicil at his death and the donee's domicil at his death are in the same state, and if the appointed property will have to be used for the payment of the donee's debts and administration expenses, then the donee's executor administers. 

§452 comment (h) Tentative Restatement of Property. The convenience and economy under this rule is somewhat offset by the fact that the rule will necessitate a proceeding in court in each case to determine the matter. (4) It has been said that this does not include a testamentary power of the bankrupt since it cannot be exercised for his own benefit and since it must be a power exercisable at the date of adjudication in bankruptcy. 

§454 comment (c) Tentative Restatement of Property. 

§199. Construction of Powers. . . . . A statement that a life tenant shall have power to appoint at his death does not require the appointment to be made by will; it merely states the time when the appointed interest is to take effect (7). The words "in default 49

-  

of appointment" are construed to mean in default of a valid appointment effecting a gift to the takers in default, of the whole or any portion of the property subject to the power as to which the donee of the power fails to make a valid appointment (8) except in the case of a general power where the donee appoints to a trustee on a trust which fails or the donee manifests an intent to assume control for all purposes (9). (4) Cf. §271 n 6 infra. 

(6) Cf. §446 comment (dd) Tentative Restatement of Property.

(8) 

See §160 at n 2; cf. §206 at n 4. (9) Then the property passes to the donee's estate. §488 Tentative 

Restatement of Property. 

$200. Exercise of Powers. In General. . . . . An appointment to a person who is dead is ineffective except that if the power is general, the appointment will be saved by the lapse statute as if the donee owned the property and had attempted to devise or bequeath it (12). 

(1) Ashton v. Wilkinson 53 E 277, 35 A 1130 E & A; §419 n 2.

(5) See §471 and §442 comment (d) Tentative Restatement of Prop erty. In Webb v. Jones 36 E 163, the donor of the power created by trust and reserved to herself was held not to have intended to exercise the power by a will drawn prior to the creation of the power. However, in another like case the donor was held to have presumably intended by her trust to permit the exercise of the power by such valid will as she might leave at her death whether the will was executed before or after the trust; and her will for its part was intended to effect any power of appointment she might have at her death. Fidelity Union Trust Co. v. Hall 125 E 419, 6 A2d 124 (where settlor of trust gave himself power by will to appoint remainder in trust; will was executed before trust; held exercise good as being incorporated into trust; see further §5 n 3); accord, see §467 Tentative Restatement of Property.

(1) Central Hanover etc. Co. v. Helme 121 E 406, 190 A 53. There fore an appointment to an object of a special power with a condition, charge or trust designed to benefit one not an object of a power or in consideration of a benefit conferred or to be conferred on a non-object, or otherwise to benefit a non-object, is ineffective to whatever extent it was designed to benefit the non-object. However, a fiduciary who carries out the appointment without notice of such ineffectiveness commits no breach of trust; and the appointment is effective as to a purchaser of the appointed property for value and without notice. §475-478 Tentative Restatement of Property. 

(2) A donee of a general power may not only appoint present or future interests, but make appointments with conditions and charges by way of trust and delegation of the power. §479 and $480 Tentative Restatement of Property. 

(3) It is said that a donee of a special power where the donor does not manifest a contrary intent may appoint present or future interests, make appointments with conditions and charges or to trustees for benefit of objects and may even to an extent delegate the power. §481 and §482 Proposed 

Final Draft No. 2 Restatement of Property, and p 147 thereof. As to dele gation of powers, cf. §275 at n 3, infra. (7) Unless the donee's scheme of disposition is more closely approximated by allowing the separable portion also to pass in default, §485 Tenta tive Restatement of Property.

$207. Construction of Conditions. 

(1) Parmentier v. Pennsylvania Co. for Ins. 122 E 25, 192 A 62, aff'd on op. 124 E 272, 1 A2d 332. (2) $140 at n 3; §195 at n 13. 

(3) §145 and §146. 

(4) Parmentier v. Pennsylvania Co. for Ins. supra n 1; §180 at n 3. 

In Joseph v. Utitz the court disregarded the gift over effective if A dies before coming into possession. But if the testator is explicit enough, particularly where there is a prior life estate and A dies during the life tenancy, the courts will effectuate a gift over. See Van Giesen v. White §209 n 8. 

Where the gift is future and not immediate, that is, where it creates a remainder or an executory interest, and it is subjected to either a condition involving the performing of a volitional act or course of conduct, e.g. drinking, smoking or abstaining therefrom, marriage, learning of a trade, attaining of wealth or solvency or supporting a person, the Restatement of Property has listed certain factors which tend to establish either that the condition is precedent or subsequent. If the condition is precedent and impossible of performance, then unless performance is excused the gift is avoided. If the condition is subsequent, impossibility leaves the gift free of the condition.

CHAPTER V PROBATE OF A WILL §216. General Nature of the Proceeding. 

(1) §222 A n 1. 

(11) Compare a probate proceeding with an accounting proceeding where informal notice is given. §364 at n 8; §366; §376. A probate proceeding on citation may be regarded as an in rem proceeding in the first three respects mentioned, except that it is not binding on the persons concerned unless citation is served on them or waived. That the proceeding is in rem, see Myers' Case 69 E 793, 796, 64 A 138, saying that whether personalty passes under a will or under a statute of distribution may be decreed without notice to interested individuals if the laws of the State permit it; and implying that the constitutional requirement of due process as to the probate of wills and distribution of an estate is satisfied by an in rem proceeding. 

§217. Jurisdiction to Probate..... If the decedent's will is en tirely revoked because of the subsequent birth of issue (10a), the court has no jurisdiction to grant probate and the probate decree is void on collateral attack (10b). For cases illustrating the principle that a decree of a court may not be attacked collaterally if the court has jurisdiction, see §231 n 12.

Where one will be injured by the probate of a will and disputes its legal existence, his objection should not be tolerated when made informally or orally. He should not be permitted to avoid the duty of causing citation to issue and bringing the case on to hearing, merely by saying "I dispute the existence of a will but will not file a caveat." Many surrogates will require of him a written caveat. Cf. §240 n 16.

$224. Effect of Probate Decree on Title to Real Estate. 

(4) 

R.S. 2:98-11 (C S 3819 §20); R.S. 2:31-21 (C S 3819 §21). See further n 6 below. (5) R.S. 3:2-11 (C S 5872 §35); R.S. 3:2-12 (P L 1916 p 234). (6) "The devisee of lands occupies no such relation to the executor ¿s that which exists between legatee and executor. No act is necessary, on the part of the executor, to put the devisee in full enjoyment of the estate devised." La Foy v. La Foy 43 E 206, 209, 10 A 266 E & A. Title passes to devisees on devisor's death. §263; Hammond v. Cronkright 47 E 447, 457, 20 A 847; Brunyate v. Chandler 99 E 67, 68, 132 A 517. So it would seem that title of realty devised to an executor in trust passes to him on devisor's death, if he accepts the trust. Contra Brown v. Pancoast 34 E 321, 327 (title in such a case passes to executor on proving the will). As to person alty, see further §232, §262. 

By R.S. 3:2-13 as amended by P L 1939 c 139, the statute provides that a will probated under R.S. 3:2-3 shall be sufficient to pass real (or personal) estate. It cannot be drawn from this language that an unprobated will shall be insufficient to pass real estate to a devisee (or to pass personalty to the executor, §262). Furthermore, it is not to be supposed that this 

1939 amendment is an implied repealer of R.S. 3:2-11, n 5 supra, on the theory that a probate proceeding now becomes conclusive before the lapse of the 7 years. The real explanation of this amendment is that it was intended to take the place of §2 of the Act of 1713 1714 (Elmer & Nixon's Digest p 1034 §37 and Revision of 1877 p 1250 §35) referred to in §224 n 4 and which was repealed by P L 1898 c 233, p 711, although relied on after 1898 by the cases that lean on Allaire v. Allaire. Despite the unfortunate conclusive-sounding language of this amendment, the courts should construe it to have but the prima facie effect given to the Act of 1713 - 1714 as stated and commented on in §224 n 4. It may be argued that since this act took effect on the same day as P L 1939 c 121, c 122 and c 140, that it was intended to make probate a condition precedent to the passage of title. But 

P L 1939 c 121, c 122 and c 140 were designed to prevent original administration and probate in a foreign state. From such a design it cannot be drawn that the law court's original jurisdiction over wills of realty was to be transferred to the probate court; such a result may not be built on a mere implication not only (a) because it would be in derogation of centuries of law to the contrary, but also (b) because it would be violative of the Constitution (§446 at n 2) to permit this jurisdiction of the law court to be handed over to the probate court. See further §232 n 1. R.S. 3:2-3 as amended P L 

1939 c 139 providing that a will to be valid must be probated, should on the above reasoning be limited to a will insofar as it deals with personalty. This statute however overturns all earlier law that an executor takes title on decedent's death. He now takes title on probate as of decedent's death.

throws upon the Orphans' Court an undefined task where original probate 61

is granted elsewhere. Is the court to require probate to be made in this state, in analogy to P L 1939 c 121, requiring original administration in this state? 

Is the statute fair process or is it capricious legislation? Is it designed solely to keep New Jersey business for New Jersey lawyers and courts? These statutes should be placed along side of P L 1939 c 123, 139 and 140. They all seemed designed partly to prevent the fraudulent avoidance of New Jersey Transfer Inheritance taxes and the avoidance of Surrogate's fees which in respect of an accounting particularly are very much larger than in other states and partly to give New Jersey lawyers business. Even P L 1939 c 

121 which makes it a "duty" to take out letters of administration goes no further than to make it a duty on "the applicant." Before one becomes bound with this "duty," he must be applying for letters; and the only place he could apply other than as provided for in the statute, would be in another state. The conclusion is plain that these statutes were not designed to require probate and administration in any case where the courts held it unnecessary before (see the express provision of P L 1939 c 122 to this effect). (2) The acts of an administrator pendente lite (pending suit) after the suit ceases may make him chargeable as an executor de son tort. §246 n 10. (3) 

R.S. 3:7-12 (C S 2260 §3). 

(4) Rennie v. Crombie 12 E 457, 473 E & A (in equity an executor de son tort is liable only to the extent of the value of the property received); 

Joss v. Mohn 55 L 407, 26 A 987 (id at law); see at n 14. R.S. 3:7-12 (C S 2260 §3). (4a) Note 4a annotates the last sentence of the first paragraph of the section. CHAPTER VII  AND ADMINISTRATORS. IN GENERAL 

$261. Functions of Executors and Administrators in General. 

Trustee Distinguished. . . . . To an extent executors and administrators continue the personality of the decedent (6). 

(1a) See §286 n 1; §387 n 1; §391. An executor becomes a trustee where there is a devise to him to sell, despite the fact that the sale is to accomplish something within the scope of an executor's duties, as to pay debts or legacies. §6 comment (c) Restatement of Trusts. But contra, indicating that the fee is annexed to the office of executor, cf. Cranstoun v. Westendorf 

91 E 34, 108 A 776 (confusing the distinction between the two offices); 

Weimar v. Fath 43 L 1; and R.S. 3:17-9. In the above case the executor has title to the land. Where there is a devise of land either (a) to be sold by the executor or (b) that he shall sell lands, he gets a "naked" power (that is, a power without any title). Cases $268 n 4; §434 n 8. See §277 n 3. A power under the statute to sell realty for debts does not confer a trust-§6 comment (c) Restatement of Trusts; neither under common usage does a "naked" power to sell for the payment of debts and legacies confer a trust. §391 at n 6. However, when a power is more extensive as when an executor is authorized to operate a business, he takes the power qua trustee. $286 n 1. 

Where there is a gift of personalty to one for life and then over, the property must be invested in the executor's name to pay the income to the life beneficiary, except where the property is specifically described in the will or there is some indication therein that the life tenant shall have the possession of the property. No distinction is perceived between such an investment in the executor's name and that of a trust. §161; §422 n 11. 

The title to all the personal property of a testator passes to his representative at his death (§262 n 1). Hence, no testamentary trustee gains title to any part thereof except with the consent of the representative (or after suit against the latter) in the manner of any legatee. As to title to realty, see §224 n 6.

Since an executor and a trustee are each entitled to commissions on all sums coming into his hands, the above analyses become important in fixing commissions. See $391. As to whether an executor or trustee distributes a remainder of personalty, see $420. 

$262. Title and Powers of Executor and Administrator Respecting Personalty.

$279. Execution of Powers. 

(1) In general, and except as provided by statute, the rules as to powers of appointment stated in §200 - §203 are applicable to powers of sale. 

Cf. Board of Home Missions etc. v. Saltmer 125 E 33, 4 A2d 69. 

(5) R.S. 37:2-1 (C S 2273 §41). 

(7a) Cf. §293 at n 8. The statutes governing investments (§292) determine several matters: (a) They not only state the form of wealth in which the estate may be laid out as a means of attaining gain; but also (b) by implication they prohibit the retention of the estate in any other form of wealth at least for a prolonged period of one to three years, except as permitted by statute, see §294 at n 11 and n 12; §230 Restatement of Trusts; and (c) the statutes also apply by implication to the acquisition of property received on the liquidation of other property even though the fiduciary may be receding from a prohibited investment. To the last matter an exception may be made to the effect that if the fiduciary can do so advantageously, he should recede along the path marked by the statutes. The acceptance of a purchase money mortgage on the sale of realty (which was either owned by the decedent or acquired by the fiduciary on the foreclosure of a mortgage) is thereby controlled by the design to be implied in the statutes. Where a fiduciary determines that a "sale could not otherwise be advantageously made" except by taking a purchase money mortgage that was not a proper investment and where the court subsequently confirms this determination as being correct, then the fiduciary is justified for having taken the mortgage. However, he acts at his peril in making this determination. The exception to the implication of the statutes to meet the case where the sale could not otherwise be advantageously made, is justified; a court may be tied by the letter of a statute but not by its own implication built up therefrom. The above analysis is supported by the Restatement of Trusts $290 comment (j) and 

Durkin v. Connelly; contra Woodruff v. Lounsberry (at the time of the

Woodruff case any type of mortgage even a second mortgage could be justified as an investment if good faith and reasonable discretion were used in selecting it §292 n 10); Farmers' Loan & Trust Co. v. Hewitt 94 E 65, 118 A 

267, aff'd on op. 94 E 187, 118 A 926 (the case may be distinguished on its special facts); cf. Cumberland Trust Co. v. Padgett; cf. Fidelity Union Trust

Co. v. Murphy 124 E 201, 1 A2d 201. 

Except in this fashion can Woodruff v. Lounsberry and Durkin v. Connelly be reconciled. The distinction with respect thereto made in the latter case cannot be supported. §296 n 3. 

In re Foundation B. & L. Assn. 122 E 549, 195 A 373 says in general that a power to sell includes by implication the power to defer payment of the purchase price and accept security therefor; and holds that trustees appointed for the liquidation of a Building and Loan Association, having power to sell under the statute dealing with such liquidation, have power to defer the payment of the purchase price and take a purchase money mortgage. The statute cited in the case gives the trustee power to divide the "money and other property among the members" (whereas an executor must reduce all property to cash, §416. Other property may perhaps be a 73

participation in a group of mortgages); and further as noted in the Foundation case, gives the trustees the powers under the constitution of the association that the board of directors had as a going concern. In the case of a going building and loan association, it is held that the power to sell lands includes the power to exchange land. Caldwell B. & L. Assn. v. Henry 120 

E 425, 185 A 394. An executor's power of sale will surely not go that far. $274. In re Foundation B. & L. Assn. is to be limited in its holding to trustees in liquidation for a building and loan association. (8) An executor has not the power to bind the estate under a warranty deed. Lindley v. Keim 54 E 418, 428 (bottom of page), 34 A 1073 E 

& A. §338 n 3. 

$280. Application of Proceeds. (2) R.S. 3:44-1 et seq. (P L 1927 p 66) Uniform Fiduciaries Act. Cf. §111 at n 7. There is another statute applicable, R.S. 3:14-2, providing that the receipt or acknowledgment in writing, by deed or otherwise, given by an executor, an administrator c. t. a. or trustee "for money paid to and received by him by reason, or in the exercise of a" trust or power shall be a full discharge; and the persons paying shall be relieved of responsibility for the application of the money unless the instrument creating the power otherwise provides. Under the quoted words of this statute it may be argued that no discharge is effected unless the fiduciary has a trust or power; and if this is so, the statute is no broader than the Uniform Fiduciaries Act.

This exemption was enacted in §290A. Investments. In General. It is not improper for a fiduciary to combine trust funds to make a single large investment where records are kept of the separate interests and the investment is otherwise proper (1). Although individual fiduciaries should not put investments in their own names without indicating their fiduciary character, unless authorized by the creator of the trust, corporate fiduciaries supervised by the banking departments will not be surcharged for making investments in their names if no harm has come thereby to the beneficiaries and if the investments are kept separate, earmarked and accounted for (2). The propriety of an investment must be judged from conditions exist- 76

ing at the time the investment is made (3). If there is a beneficiary entitled to income, the investment must be made by a trustee so that it yields an income to the beneficiary (4) not substantially less than the current return on trust investments (5). $294. Continuance of Decedent's Investments. . . . . This statute does not limit the length of time during which a fiduciary may retain such an investment except as contained in the words "reasonable discretion" (2). 

(1) R.S. 3:16-12 (C S 2271 §34 P L 1899 c 103 §1). Under $3 of act 

(P L 1899 c 103 §3) §1 of the act (now R.S. 3:16-12) did not apply where the will specially directed in what manner the estate should be invested. For example if the will expressly directed investments in "legals" it was thought 78

that the estate should be liquidated and reinvested in "legals" as soon as reasonably possible. §294 at n 12. For it would be too narrow to hold that a testator's direction as to the making of investments did not control the continuance of his investments. §292 n 24. The revisers have omitted §3 of the original act so that now even if the testator directs the sale of all his securities at his death, the executor may perhaps continue them. It will be argued that there is nothing for the court to say but that the omission was intentional, §307 B n 5. On the other hand the court's whole impulse will be to construe the statute as but a presumptive rule of construction; and this impulse should prevail. 

It has been held in Chancery, although the matter has been expressly left open in the Court of Errors, that a trustee of a pecuniary legacy may under this statute accept stocks in testator's estate in satisfaction of the legacy, if he acts in good faith and with due care. In re Ward 121 E 

555, 192 A 68 (Ch), 121 E 606, 191 A 772 E & A. See In re Brown 112 E 

499, 164 A 692; §416 at n 3. So a trustee of the residuary estate can continue an investment of the decedent. See e.g. In re Riker 124 E 228, 1 A2d 

213, afí'd on op. 125 E 349, 350, 351, 5 A2d 685; In re Griggs 125 E 73, 4 

A2d 59; Parker v. Glover 42 E 559, Brown v. Brown 72 E 667, 678; Beam v. Paterson Safe Dep. and Trust Co. 83 E 628; §416 n 3. The statute specifically applies to a trustee under a will, and since no trustee can acquire title to personalty except as a beneficiary, to wit through the executor (§262 n 1; §261 n 1a) it seems plain that all trustees whether they be trustees of a pecuniary legacy or of the residuary estate can continue testator's investment assuming that the statute is out, by presumptive rule of construction as above argued. It cannot be said that the testator in the case of a trust of a pecuniary legacy has provided against the continuance of his investments. 

The sum of dollars measures the amount of the investment; by a mention of a sum of money the testator did not have in mind the kind of securities in which the investment was to be made. The gift of a pecuniary legacy in trust is almost entirely a device for describing a legacy having preference over the residue; the indication from these words of the will that the executor is to reduce to cash the gift to the trustee is altogether too slight, having regard to this primary purpose. (3) The statute has been construed with such an unjustified generosity to the fiduciary that it is said in the lay talk of corporate fiduciaries "testator's investments are legals." (9) Where testator holds stock in a corporation that is merged or reorganized into a new corporation, his corporate fiduciary can receive and retain new shares issued for the old, provided the new shares are substantially the equivalent of the old. In re Riker 124 E 228, 1 A2d 213. The transaction is not voidable even though the trust is of stock in the corporate fiduciary. Id. (10) Where the holding company, a fire insurance company offers to the fiduciary its stock in exchange for the stock held by the decedent in a casualty company the fiduciary is not authorized by this statute to make the exchange as a continuance of the decedent's investment. In re Riker 124 

E 228, 1 A2d 213.

(4) Cf. In re Riker 124 E 228, 1 A2d 213, aff'd on op. 125 E 349, 350, 351, 5 A2d 685. (5) McAllister v. McAllister 120 E 407, 184 A 723, aff'd on op. 121 E 

264, 190 A 52. Such investments are voidable at the option of the beneficiaries. So investments made by a trustee in participation certificates issued by itself. In re Shaw 122 E 536, 195 A 525. Nor can it purchase securities from an affiliated or subsidiary company. Gates v. Plainfield Trust Co.
