§307A. Actions by and against Executors and Administra tors (1). For either an executor or an administrator to sue or be sued, letters must have been issued to him in this state (2). 

Under the rules of both the Supreme Court (3) and the Court of Chancery (4) claims by or against any executor or administrator, as such, must not, without leave of court (5), be joined with claims by or against him personally unless the latter claims arose with reference to the estate of his testator or intestate (6). Where in Chancery a beneficiary sues, the representative being made a defendant because he failed to sue (7) and where one cause of action is to compel a recovery from a third party and the other cause of action is against the executor individually, the rules are not violated as there is no claim against the representative as such 

In connection with other matters there is taken up the manner of dealing with claims by an individual against himself in a representative capacity and claims against an individual by one in his representative capacity (9); the questions as to whether rights or liabilities are acquired or incurred in a person.1 or representative capacity (10); and the effect of death on the Statute of Limitations and the commencement of suits and the power of the representative to waive the Statute of Limitations (11).

Creditors actions are considered in §313 - §361 (12). 

Else- where also are considered the effect of a judgment entered against a decedent in his lifetime (13) and the effect of a judgment entered against his heirs or personal representatives (14). 

By statute (15) if a judgment debtor dies, then six months after his death, execution may, if the court so orders, issue at law on ten days' notice as provided by the statute, as if death had not occurred, against decedent's personal and real estate. If objection. 8883

-  

is made by an interested party and letters testamentary or of administration have been granted, then no order may be made until three months after notice of the existence of the judgment was first given the executor or administrator. The statutes (16) provide for issuance of an execution at law in favor of the survivors of several judgment creditors and against the survivors of several judgment debtors; and in the case where the sole judgment creditor dies (17) his death may be suggested on the record and execution may issue against his executor or administrator. (1) See §313 for the payment, set-off, compromise and arbitration of claims against the estate and for the payment of judgments against the rep resentatives. The statute (R.S. 3:15-1 et seq. commented on in §313 at n

This, however, does not protect the one owing the claim and assure him of the authority of the representative to compromise. Such authority as well as authority to arbitrate, set-off and release claims by the representative may be gathered by way of analogy to cases cited in §313. §307B. Survival of Actions. The question whether an action 

84

survives arises on the death of the person entitled to the cause of action and also on the death of the person against whom the cause of action lies (1). At common law the general rule was that if either party died, no action could be supported by or against his personal representatives when the action was in form ex delicto and the plea not guilty (2). All other actions survived. 

By statute the common law was changed so that any action belonging to a decedent based upon a delictual act to his "person or property" committed in New Jersey (3) survives to his executors or administrators (4), except a type of action for which he could have recovered punitive damages. For a period from December 20, 1937 to March 16, 1938 this statute was by error changed so as to eliminate the words "person or"; and during that period only property damage to a decedent survived (5). Also by statute any cause of action based upon a delictual act committed in New Jersey (6) by a decedent survives against his executors or administrators (7) except a type of action for which punitive damages could have been recovered against him.

Hence, under the statutes, in actions where the damages may be punitive for wounded pride and feelings and not limited to compensation, the action does not survive either in favor of, or against, the representatives of a decedent; as, for example, an action for slander, libel, assault and battery (8). All other causes of action now survive (9). Equity will, without doubt, follow the law (10).

If an action survives as against his personal representative under the statute above mentioned, it survives as against his heirs, devisees (11) and beneficiaries. (1) This is a question of substantive law. There is a procedural question as to the abatement of an action at law or suit in equity on the death of the plaintiff, the complainant or the defendant and as to the manner of its revivor. These procedural questions arise when death occurs after an action or suit has been started by or against said party; and lie in the field of law and chancery practice, beyond the scope of this book. See in general 

R.S. 2:26-4 through R.S. 2:26-8; and R.S. 2:26-11 through R.S. 2:26-26 and

Chancery Rules 62-65. 

(2) Ten Eyck v. Runk 31 L 428. This is a free rendition of the Latin phrase "actio personalis mortitur cum persona." Cooper v. Shore Electric 

Co. 63 L 558, 44 A 633 E & A; Hayden v. Vreeland 37 L 372. Conversely to the rule in the text, it is said with respect to claims on any obligation, contract debt, covenant or other duty, that a right of action on which a decedent might have been sued in his lifetime survives his death at common law and is enforceable against his executor or administrator. McCarthy v. 

Mullen 82 L 379, 381, 82 A 51 E & A. This common law rule was extended early by statute, 4 Edw III c 7 (1331), to cover actions of trespass against 85

the decedent and by construction of that statute with 25 Edw III c 5 to actions of case and assumpsit. Ten Eyck v. Runk supra; Cooper v. Shore 

Electric Co. supra; Soden v. Trenton etc. Traction Corp. 101 L 393, 127 A 

558 E & A. The terms of these statutes are substantially engrossed in our statutes referred to at n 4 and n 7 infra. (3) Rathgeber v. Sommerhalder 112 L 546, 171 A 835 E & A; Friedman v. Greenberg 110 L 462, 166 A 119 E & A. (4) $284. C S 2260 84 was construed to include an action for pain and suffering to the decedent (Ryan v. Public Service Ry. Co. 103 L 145, 134 A 650 E & A) as well as to an action for expense of care, nursing, medical attendance, hospital charges, loss of earnings and other items of financial loss. Soden v. Trenton etc. Traction Corp. 101 L 393, 127 A 558 E 

& A. The extension of the word "trespass," found in the statute, to cover the latter items can be justified on precedent going back to Mansfield. See n 7 infra. To be differentiated from the actions which survive in favor of the personal representative for damage suffered by the decedent, is the death action whereby a new cause of action is raised by statute for the benefit of the surviving spouse and next of kin for pecuniary damage suffered by them. R.S. 2:47. 

(5) De Luca v. Bodman 17 M 1, 4 A2d 774; Saslow v. Previti 17 M 

29, 3 A2d 811; R.S. 2:26-9 effective December 20, 1927 was corrected by P L 1938 p 103 effective March 16, 1938. See §392 n 1, R.S. 1:1-4. (6) See n 3 supra. (7) R.S. 2:26-10. The statute uses the word "trespass" but the word is to be construed broadly to mean tort. Hackensack Trust Co. v. Vanden 

Berg 88 L 518, 97 A 148, appeal dismissed in 92 L 412, 105 A 719 (the word includes a right to recover under the Death Act); Newman v. Sanders 

89 L 120, 97 A 782 (action for permissive waste survives); Alpaugh v. Conkling 88 L 64, 95 A 618 (trespass means tort); Pushcart v. N. Y. Shipbuilding 

Co. 86 L 444, 92 A 81; Crane v. Ketcham 83 L 327, 331, 84 A 1052; Ten 

Eyck v. Runk 31 L 428; Cooper v. Shore Electric Co. 63 L 588, 44 A 633

E & A; see n 4 supra. The statute covers by its terms, personal as well as proprietal trespass. Cf. Weller v. Jersey City etc. Ry. Co. 68 E 659, 61 A 

459 E & A; Goldfarb v. Reicher 112 L 413, 171 A 149, aff'd on op. 113 L 

399, 174 A 507. The courts have gone beyond Mansfield's thought that the word "trespass" in this statute is limited to an act resulting in financial detriment. For Mansfield's thought, see Hayden v. Vreeland 37 L 372;

Cooper v. Shore Electric Co. 63 L 558, 44 A 633 E & A. The word is held to include suffering, see Ryan v. Public Service Ry. Co. 103 L 145, 134 A 

650 E & A. The statute itself covers a tort to the person of the decedent as well as to his property. (8) Hayden v. Vreeland 37 L 372 (a case of breach of promise of marriage). In Alpaugh v. Conkling 88 L 64, 95 A 618 (Sup. Ct.), the court held that a right of action for slander (dictum in accord as to libel) does not survive as against the personal representatives of the tort feasor in the absence of any special damage to property rights, implying that for a suit to revive there must be an injury to property rights. (In that implication the case followed Cooper v. Shore Electric Co. supra n 7 which had limited the broad dicta of Noice v. Brown 39 L 569 (an action for seduction) that 86

even actions involving punitive damages survived). Actions for pain and suffering caused by decedent in an auto accident survive (see Ryan v. Public 

Service Ry. Co. supra n 7), a distinction being drawn between suffering caused to pride and self respect and bodily suffering. The rule put forth in the text is a reconciliation of the law-but see Souvay v. Fricke 11 M 882, 169 A 33 (alienation of affections); Meyer v. Peter 9 M 1309, 157 A 250 (malicious prosecution); and cf. Messina v. Petroli 11 M 583, 167 A 767 (general administrator may sue for punitive damages from an assault). (9) For non-delictual acts, see McCarthy v. Mullen 82 L 379, 381, 82 A 51 E & A. (10) Since equity generally deals with property rights, suits there will generally survive. (11) Primmer v. Baldwin 1 M 95. 

$307C. Survival of Joint Causes of Action on Contract (1). 

This section deals first with the case where one of two persons jointly bound on a contract dies. At common law the whole responsibility devolved upon the surviving obligor and on his death on his personal representative (2). But by statute (3) the representatives of one jointly bound with another under any contract may be charged as if the obligation had been joint and several (4), unless the estate of the decedent is in settlement as an insolvent estate (5). The result of the statute is that the representatives of a deceased joint obligor are liable to a surviving co-obligor, for contribution of an aliquot part of what he has paid. 

At common law in analogy to survivorship in joint estates, if one of two persons jointly entitled to personalty as a contractual right dies, the right vests in the survivor and on his death in his representative (6). But now it is said in the rules of courts that in suits on a joint contract the personal representatives of the decedent may join as plaintiffs or complainants (7). A right to join as a party carries a clear implication of a substantive right; but it is questionable whether such a right can be conferred by court rules which can only regulate procedure and practice (8). (1) The question of this section rarely arises in tort cases for persons bound delictually are liable (a) jointly and severally, or (b) severally but, except in rare cases, not jointly alone. Cf. Newman v. Fowler 37 L 89; cf. §310 n 11. And persons entitled to an action rarely have no several rights thereto. 

(2) Sigler v. Interest 3 L *724; Hedden v. Van Ness 2 L *84. If the contract is personal in character requiring the action of all obligors, then the death of one discharges all from liability. Williston on Contracts §344. (3) R.S. 2:70-2. Beyond this statute, there is no remedy in equity to treat a joint obligation as several unless (a) the agreement may be reformed to that effect, or (b) the deceased joint obligor shared in the consideration for which the obligation was given. Dixon v. Vandenberg 35 E 47; Willis- 87

-  

ton on Contracts §344; cf. Stothoff v. Dunham's Ex'rs 19 L 181; Hazen v.

Durling 2 E 133. 

(4) Thompson v. Johnson 40 L 220; Fleming v. Reed 77 L 563, 72 A 

299 E & A. As a result, the liability of the representative of a decedent with the survivor is several, not joint. Disborough v. Heirs of Bidleman 21 

L 677 E & A; Wade v. Potter 14 L 278, 281; Corlies v. Fleming 30 L 349; 

Wilson v. Hughes 119 E 175, 177, 181 A 649; Parker v. Butterworth 46 L

§323. Secured Creditors and Holders of Property Rights. 

Where one has an equitable title or interest in property subject to which the decedent has an interest, the former's interest therein is superior to the rights of decedent's preferred creditors respecting it. . . .

A general creditor has no lien on the personal assets of the estate unless it be insolvent (4). Unless a creditor has a lien because of the insolvency of the estate or otherwise, or unless there be some special equity in his favor as in a proceeding to set aside a bulk sale (5) he may not sue in equity to attack a chattel mortgage (6) or a fraudulent conveyance of such personalty (7). $330. Remedies against Decedent's Lands by a Charge under the Will. . . . But the lien cannot be enforced if there has been a bona fide alienation and a year has elapsed from the testator's death (13).

An executor's deed under a power of sale discharges the lien created by the will and the purchaser need not see to the application of the proceeds of sale (14). (4) $180 at n 9. (7a) $326 at n 13. As to an insolvent estate, see §361 n 3. (8) At least the residuary personalty is the primary fund. §106. (12) The effect of the decisions cited in §330 n 12 was nullified by the statute now R.S. 3:25-22, §332 (passed in the year after the decision of 

First Nat'l Bank v. Tobin) which is interpreted to mean that lands shall be liable for debts for one year after the testator's death and thereafter until a bona fide alienation, and which provides that the time so fixed in this statute shall not be deemed to be extended by language in the will that debts be paid or by any language that does not expressly extend the lien. When the statute provides that the duration of the statutory lien shall not be extended by a testamentary direction to pay debts, the legislature crudely but clearly stated that the charge created by the testamentary direction should, unless the testator otherwise provides, be limited to the one year and thereafter until a bona fide sale is made. §353. Remedies of Barred Creditors against Representative. 

Against Such Surplus Where There is no Account. 

(1) R.S. 3:25-15 (C S 3837 §77). The statute (R.S. 3:25-15) is super fluous. Where there is an account filed, the creditor may proceed under 

R.S. 3:25-11 (§352). No distinction is perceived between (a) surplus in a representative's hands and (b) a legacy or distributive share in his hands. 

When there is no account filed, the first step is for the creditor to compel an account because no action may be brought under R.S. 3:25-15 until an account is filed. O'Neil v. Freeman 45 L 208; Cunningham v. Stanford 68

L 7, 52 A 374. And if an account is to be filed, he may proceed under R.S. 

3:25-16. The only significance that can be forced upon this statute (R.S. 

3:25-15) is in the matter of costs outside the probate court. When the creditor's claim is disputed and he must proceed at law or equity to establish it, it may be held that he will get no costs in law or equity if he recovers, whether he is proceeding under R.S. 3:25-11 or R.S. 3:25-16, the costs referred to in those latter two statutes being the costs of the Orphans' Court. 

It appears that R.S. 3:25-15 was designed to avoid the necessity of an accounting, but the courts rejected such a design. If this analysis is correct, the Revisers should have omitted the section except as to costs at law and equity. (3) There must be a final judicial settlement embracing not only the payment of debts but also commissions and administrative expenses, even though there appears to be ample funds for these purposes. Philadelphia etc. Society v. Disston 16 M 129, 137, 198 A 212. (4) See §365 as to which court will take jurisdiction of an account. (5) R.S. 3:25-16 (P L 1920 p 608). The creditor can under no statute compel an account prior to the termination of one year after letters, so the creditor need but look to this statute for the accounting. §368. 

(6) R.S. 3:25-16 (P L 1920 p 608). In the Revised Statutes 1937, the 

Prerogative Court is expressly included with the Orphans' Court. It is not perceived why the statute provides for the situation where an account has been filed but no decree of distribution made. The provision would seem superfluous as R.S. 3:25-11 covers the case. As to the obligation to make a decree of distribution, see §415 n 4, and further see §426. 

(10) Philadelphia etc. Society v. Disston 16 M 129, 198 A 212. 

(11) R.S. 3:25-20 (C S 3838 §79).

§366. Parties in Chancery.. When an executor or admin istrator files a bill in chancery to have his accounts allowed he becomes, to speak, a defendant as well as a complainant. Both the complainant and the defendants to the suit are actors. If the balance is ultimately found due a defendant, it will be so decreed; any counterclaim for such a balance is unnecessary and will be struck (3). . . . .

$372. Petition, Notice, Guardians ad Litem and Audit of Accounts..... Although the matter is not free from doubt, accounting proceedings are deemed to be in rem (12a) and, hence, conclusive on all persons whether sui juris or not (12b). Without considering this (12c), the Court of Chancery once said that an infant interested in an accounting decree could void it on coming of age (12d); and while this rule remains unimpeached by the Court of Chancery or the Court of Errors, the practice contrary to the former practice (12e) is to appoint a guardian ad litem (12f). On the settlement of the account good practice asks the guardian to file a report in writing stating the interest of his ward, and stating that he examined the assets and securities, if he deems it advisable, and that he examined the inventory, appraisement, account, vouchers, list of assets and statement of changes in securities. He must have the law examined with respect thereto (12g). If he finds no ground for filing an exception to either the inventory or account, he should so report. He should specify in his report disputable items so that the court's attention may be called to them, even though he concludes them to be proper.

(14) Further consideration indicates that a mistake of law should not be ground for setting aside a decree of the Orphans' or Prerogative Court where the time for appeal has expired. Under the case of Miller v. Mc- 

Cutcheon 117 E 123, 175 A 155, §458 n 11, a change of law arising by reason of a superior court overruling its earlier decisions is not a sufficient ground to open a decree made by an inferior court after the time for appeal has expired. It may be reasoned, therefore, that any change of view on the law by any court does not constitute ground for it to open its decree after that period has expired. It is true that the cases, §378 n 9 n 11, were cases 107

of mistakes of law more obvious than those in Miller v. McCutcheon; but the obviousness of the error can be no practicable test for an exception. (16) $458 n 12.

(1) R.S. 3:11-2 (C S 3860 $129). As to what administration expenses may be charged to the estate, see §320. (2) The Revised Statutes 1937 only limit the amount of commissions on accounts in the Orphans' and Prerogative Courts. 

(3) Under R.S. 3:11-2 (C S 3860 §129), as amended P L 10 c 134, the statement of the text is only true as to corpus. Under the amendment commissions on income may be taken by the fiduciary as of the time the income was received or at the time it is received without the allowance of court. Prior to the taking effect of this amendment on July 1, 1939, income had to be paid to a cestui without deduction for commissions until commissions were allowed and settled by court. Lathrop v. Smalley's Ex'rs 23 E 

192; McKnight's Ex'rs v. Walsh 23 E 136, 149, aff'd 24 E 498. §176 n 2.

But in the case of corpus commissions, even where the testator or settlor provides as to them, the fiduciary is not permitted to collect except on an accounting and order of a competent court. If, however, the beneficiaries consent to the payment of such commissions without court allowance, then the fiduciary will certainly not be surcharged for commissions where he has not disentitled himself to them. McAllister v. McAllister 120 E 407, 423, 184 A 723, aff'd on op. 121 E 264, 190 A 52. (4) and (5) See n 3. (9) In re Kraeuter 125 E 120, 4 A2d 383.

$389. Commissions on Income. By a recent amendment (1) 

the law is sufficiently changed as to require this entire section to be rewritten. Whether or not there are provisions of a will or directions of a court requiring property to remain in a fiduciary's hands to pay the income to a beneficiary (2), a fiduciary is entitled to take on all income that comes into his hands (3) 5% thereof (4) either when the income is received or at the option of the fiduciary 109

-  

as of the time the income is received without allowance thereof by court (5).

Commissions on income must be paid out of income (6). Where a gift is of an annuity as distinguished from the income of a fund (7) or if it appears that the testator intended the income to be paid clear of charges, then commissions are not to be deducted therefrom (8).

§406. Illegitimate Children. 

(2) R.S. 3:3-10 (P L 1930 p 568); R.S. 9:15-1 (P L 1915 p 333); R.S. 

3:5-7 (P L 1930 p 394). The gift of the realty to the next of kin is unhappy. 

Query as to its operation. See §59 n 19; §405 at n 8. Clearly the words "next of kin" have some meaning and possibly they were inserted to give the spouse of the illegitimate child a share. Under $307 Tentative Restatement of Property when realty is limited by one in favor of the next of kin of another, then the realty goes to those entitled to such other's personalty as if he died intestate. Surely the takers of intestate personalty will not be excluded where the limitation is "to heirs and next of kin." If such is the construction to be put on a limitation, should not a statute be construed similarly? Suppose a mother survives her illegitimate child, X, and both are survived by X's spouse and child A. If X's spouse is to be included as a next of kin (as he or she should, cf. §60), then either (a) the heirs could take one-half and the next of kin one-half, giving A 5/6 and the spouse 1/6 on the assumption that the intent of the statute was not to benefit equally the individuals who comprise the two groups, but rather to benefit 112

equally the two groups (cf. §311 Tentative Restatement of Property), the amounts of the respective shares being determined according to statutory proportions; or (b) the spouse and A could each take one-half on the assumption that the takers fixed by the intestacy statutes take per capita; or (c) some other construction might be made.

Under this statute (R.S. 3:3-10) maternal grandparents will take as heirs of an illegitimate child.

§413. Distribution to other Next of Kin..... It will be perceived that this is not a rule that those take who are representatives of ancestors nearest decedent (6); although in the case given, 113

such a rule would bring the same result. If by chance in that case, one of the great uncles did survive decedent, he and the issue of all decedent's great uncles, paternal and maternal, would take along with the living first cousins and the issue of deceased cousins; and if that great uncle survived and all first cousins predeceased decedent leaving issue surviving him, then the surviving great uncle and the issue of deceased great uncles take it all. (1) R.S. 3:5-6 as amended P L 1939 c 256 (P L 1930 p 394). Vanderveer's Adm'r 23 E 558 E & A. (5) "First cousins" and "great uncles" do not answer the same description. In the case given, it is assumed that no great great grandparent is alive. $418. Legacies Draw Interest from One Year after Testator's 

Death. .... The rule is that interest runs on all general legacies (except residuary legacies) from one year after the testator's death (1) at the legal rate of 6% (2). (1) If a pecuniary legatee received no interest the residuary legatee would profit by the income received on the amount of the former's legacy. 

The rule does not apply to a residuary legacy given outright (nor to the principal of a residuary legacy given for life and over, cf. §419 n 9) because (a) it would be a matter of surcharging the representative and he is only chargeable for bad faith and negligence, §302; and, furthermore (b) the residuary legatee gets the income earned on his share. But contra, In re 

Ferguson 124 E 573, 3 A2d 439 (where the representative acted negligently not only with regard to the $2100.00 counsel fee paid but also in not paying over the balance of the residuary estate amounting to $2728.81, §417 at n 7). 

The cases are so far as presently observed all cases of pecuniary legacies; but the principle is equally applicable e.g. to a general legacy of stock.

(12) An option holder has an interest in land subject to a contingent condition precedent that he exercise the option. Why should not this contingent right to get land pass to the heir or devisee on the option holder's 120

This may death if a vested right would go to him on a purchaser's death? be explained on the "unjust enrichment theory" (see authorities n 10 §440); but it may also be explained on the "in rem" theory. The contingency is in an event, not "in the person" of the holder of the right. A contingent interest in land where the contingency is in the event and not in the person, is transmissible by will or by the intestacy statutes. §134. See §188 n 5. (13) If the option went to the personal representative of the purchaser and on the exercise of the option the lands went to the heirs or devisees, the option would never be exercised, as the representative (and so the beneficiaries of the personalty) would have to pay for the land and the heirs or devisees would get it. (14) leaving to the personal representative, the duty to pay for the land. It may be held that the statute (R.S. 3:23-6) providing that an executor and administrator may carry into effect decedent's agreement for the purchase or sale of land, applies to an option; and that in the case of an option-contract for the purchase of land, as well as in the case of a contract where no option is involved, the lands will go, after the payment of debts, to the takers of the real estate. Cf. §285 n 7. In that case the takers of the realty should have the right to exercise the option. See n 13. 

(15) Greenbaum v. Austrian 70 Ill 591.

8444. Effects of Conversion. . . . . Under the doctrine of con version realty is fictitiously regarded as personalty and personalty as realty, solely for the purposes of devolution as of the date of the death of the owner thereof. There is no relation back of the right of the beneficiaries of the conversion (8) for any other purposes as to collect the rents of realty to be converted or to prevent the alienation of the property. However, when realty is to be converted by a direction under a will, the purchaser takes his title from the testator as of his death; but this is under the doctrine of powers and not of conversion (9). (4) i.e. From the time of the actual conversion, the title of the purchaser relates back to the death of the devisor, getting the full title of the devisor. §268 at n 10; §435 at n 4 et seq.

CHAPTER XI PROBATE COURTS AND PRACTICE IN GENERAL, INCLUD- ING JURISDICTION, COSTS AND REVIEW OF DECREES 

$445. Jurisdiction. In General.....There is no federal jurisdiction over probate matters (19) except matters determinable by a civil action either in the nature of a suit in chancery or an action at law. (1) The word is frequently used to describe a situation where the court has jurisdiction but will not exercise it. (3) Acquiescence will not confer jurisdiction in this sense. 

Struble's Estate 87 E 311, 101 A 177.

In re (6) Clark Ex'r v. Clark 2 L 112. In a petition without an answer the issues may be defined by the prayer in the petition. Trenton Trust Co. v. Gane 125 E 389, 392, 6 A2d 112. (7) Romanchick v. Howard Savings Institution 118 L 606, 194 A 185 E & A; Fidelity Union Trust Co. v. Ackerman 121 E 497, 191 A 813, mod. 123 E 

556, 199 A 379. Where letters testamentary or of administration issued by a court are void because of its lack of jurisdiction, the court will not protect bona fide purchasers from the representatives and debtors of the estate paying their debts in good faith. Romanchick v. Howard Savings Institution 118 L 606, 194 A 185 E & A; 32 Harvard Law Review 328. However where the letters are not void because the court had jurisdiction, such purchasers and debtors will be protected: e.g. where administration has been granted and a valid will is later probated or where one will is probated and a valid subsequent will is probated (§234 n 2); or where letters are issued on the estate of one legally presumed to be dead who is in fact alive (§248 n 4a). For jurisdiction to grant probate, see §217. For jurisdiction to issue letters testamentary or of administration, see §231. $448. Particular Limitations on the Jurisdiction of the Pro bate Courts.....Nor has the probate court power to grant specific performance (18). 

(2) R.S. 2:97-1 (C S 2218 §3). Under the Revised Statutes this act now applies to any action, proceeding or matter of a civil or criminal nature --and not merely to suits at law or equity. (8a) This note annotates the last sentence in the second paragraph of 

§448. The statute granting the Orphans' Court power to issue an injunction 

(§451 n 28) was passed P L 1855 p 342 §15 (see Hunt v. Mayberry 29 L 403).

The question of the text is whether this statute permitting an injunction infringed on Chancery's power and was therefore unconstitutional. See §446 at n 2 and n 17. Prior to the Constitution of 1844, the Orphans' Court had undoubted power to punish with contempt, disobedience to its orders. §451 n 15. 

Since then its power to issue a mandatory injunction has been confirmed by the courts. See Dietz's Case 41 E 284, 298, 7 A 443, aff'd on op. 

42 E 689, 10 A 795 abstracted §283 n 3; Sherman v. Lanier 39 E 249 (the court may compel a defaulting trustee to turn over trust funds to a successor trustee). The power of the Orphans' Court to issue a restraining or a mandatory injunction must obviously be limited to the effectuation of probate jurisdiction. But, subject to such limitation, such power must have been inherent in the court at the time of the Constitution of 1844, as either necessarily (§446 at n 17) to be implied from the contempt statute or as inherent in a court of general jurisdiction. Query whether the same argument applies to a writ of ne exeat. (10) In re Struble's Estate 87 E 311, 101 A 177 (an administrator cannot be compelled to account for rents he has collected). (11) The probate court may not impress a lien.

Koch v. Feick 81 E 

123

120, A 67 (Orphans' Court has no power to declare a constructive trust over personalty); Sherman v. Lanier 39 E 249 (the Orphans' Court cannot decree that on a defaulting trustee's payment of amount due by her, the succeeding trustee shall reconvey the property to her). Further as to constructive trust, see §12 n 5. Nor has the Orphans' Court the power to establish a resulting trust as to a cemetery lot. In re McConnell 105 E 687, 149 A 352. 

(12) PL 1938 c 108 gives the Prerogative and Orphans' Courts powers over accounts of non-testamentary trustees. It is said that this statute is not unconstitutional as infringing on Chancery's jurisdiction (§446 at n 2) because under an unreported decision of Backes, V. C., Chancery has no jurisdiction over accounts of trustees made voluntarily and on their own application. Query. The Orphans' Court has taken jurisdiction under this statute, of accounts of persons formerly directors acting as trustees in dissolution of a corporation where there were no creditors concerned, despite the fact that there was no writing creating the trust other than the stockholders' consent to dissolution. On the other hand, the Orphans' Court has refused jurisdiction in the case of an oral resulting trust. (14) §325 n 2; In re Brueck 124 E 62, 199 A 61 E & A; In re Konigsberg 125 E 216, 4 A2d 524; but to the contrary cf. Ferdon's Case 69 E 762, 62 A 551; cf. §363 at n 3. R.S. 3:13-10 (C S 3865 §139). 

(16) $325 n 2. Claims may be litigated in the Probate Court also where the representative admits a claim of another whether or not he pays it. §325 at n 3. He renders himself liable to the claimant by such an admission (§370 at n 14); and the litigation of his right of indemnification becomes a matter over which the probate court will take jurisdiction. Cf. 

§287 at n 12; §315. 

(18) Sherman v. Lanier 39 E 249, n 11 supra; cf. §285 at n 9. $449. "Incidental" Jurisdiction. (1) In re McConnell 105 E 687, 149 A 352. $450. The Prerogative Court. . . . . There is a disposition on the part of the Prerogative Court to refuse to grant probate or administration where there is an adequate remedy in the Surrogate's Office (28). The statutes frequently provide in respect of probate proceedings arising in a cause after probate or administration has been granted (29) that such proceedings be had (a) in the Prerogative Court provided administration or probate was granted there or (b) in the Orphans' Court, provided administration or probate was granted by the Surrogate or Orphans' Court. Even where these statutes do not control, the rule at least generally is that the Prerogative Court will not exercise jurisdiction in the case where probate or administration was not granted in that court (30) unless the remedy in the Orphans' Court is inadequate. On the other hand where probate or administration has been granted in the Prerogative Court, the Orphans' Court will not exercise jurisdiction of such proceedings (31).

$451. Orphans' Court. . . . . The rules of the Orphans' Court made by the Prerogative Court are as the rules of all courts, rigidly adhered to, except when they will work an injustice. Where a litigant is in default, then he must, in order to obtain from the court a relaxation of the rules, show not only an excuse for his default, but also a meritorious case. Strangely it appears that not only the Prerogative Court on appeal but also the Orphans' Court may relax the latter's Rules fixed for it by the former court under a statutory grant (31). The Orphans' Court has the inherent power to make rules governing its own procedure provided there is no conflict with the rules made for it by the Prerogative Court (32). Elsewhere are considered the matter of the jurisdiction of the Orphans' Court of one county to grant probate or administration over estates of those dying resident in this state but non-resident in the county (33) and the matter of its jurisdiction in administration proceedings subsequent to probate or administration granted in the Prerogative 

Court (34).

$453. Costs and Counsel Fees in General. 

(2) The statutes completely cover the matter of costs and counsel fees in the Orphans' Court except in the case of a fund in court (see §457), and except in the probate of a will. And in the last case the statute has codified the earlier law (see §456 at n 19a).

There is no statute expressly governing the Prerogative Court (except in connection with the probate of a will, §456) but since that court follows Chancery practice (§450 at n 27) it may be contended that the Chancery statutes (R.S. 2:29-129 et seq.) obtain where the Prerogative Court is exercising original jurisdiction or where it is concerned on appeal with a matter which it may hear de novo. Cf. §456 n 1. These Chancery acts give a wider latitude than the somewhat circumscribed powers of the Orphans' Court stated in §454 - §457. Clearly the Chancery statutes do not apply to other appellate proceedings in the Prerogative Court. Cases $453 n 2; In re Mc- 

Cabe 124 E 254, 1 A2d 411 E & A (commented on in §456 n 3). In these last proceedings no costs may be allowed except those provided for in the fee bill, and except on the probate of a will or where there is a fund in court. 

In re Queen 82 E 588, 89 A 860. As to the fee bill, R.S. 22:2-29 states that 128

fees are allowed for like services as in Chancery. For other cases in the 

Prerogative Court, see Skillman v. Skillman 15 E 388 (costs on appeal to Prerogative Court denied each party as against the other and as against the estate); In re Dreier's Estate 83 E 618, 92 A 51 (id). The court may require the estate to pay costs of printing record where appellant claims she will be unable to make payment until the case is decided. On the decision no costs of appeal were allowed either party and each was required to pay half the cost of printing. Kinnan v. Wight 40 E 531, 4 A 777. Costs as to the taking of testimony de novo on appeal will be deferred until the decree, being then dependent on whether the party taking them was in laches for not having done so in the Orphans' Court. In re Thompson 10 N J L J 209. (4) For the statutes cited in this note, see R.S. 22:2-37 (C S 3885

L 1914 p 215, P L 1915 p 326); R.S. 22:2-38 - 22:2-39 (P L 1928 p 315). (5) For the statutes cited in this note, see R.S. 22:2-31 (C S 1724

§462. Appeal from Orphans' Court to Prerogative Court. In 

General. . . . . By the statute (4) such an appeal, unless otherwise especially provided by order of the Prerogative Court (4a), must in all cases be taken by filing a written (5) notice of appeal with the Surrogate under the Rules (6) within thirty days after the Orphans' Court decree is reduced to writing, signed and filed or entered upon the minutes of the court (7-8). (2) R.S. 2:31-93 (C S 3889 §204). (3) In general, see Black v. Kirgan 15 L 45; In re Van Riper's Estate 

90 E 217, 220, 107 A 55. As to Young's case, cited in this note, see §221 n 

13. An insurance company not being bound by an adjudication of death is not a person entitled to appeal. Moyna v. Prudential Life Ins. Co. 96 E 

293, 125 A 99 E & A. A stranger to the suit cannot appeal. Rorback v. 

Van Blarcom 20 E 461; Swackhamer v. Kline's Adm'r 25 E 503; Middleditch v. Williams 47 E 585, 21 A 290. An appeal will not lie on a decree entered on appellant's motion and in his favor. In re McCabe 124 E 254, 1 A2d 411 E & A.

(3) See Podesta v. Moody 69 E 468, 60 A 939. An appeal lies from an interlocutory order, see §468 n 1. But the interlocutory order referred to in the text is of the sort that lies in the absolute discretion of the court. (4) In re Braunstein's Will 105 E 682, 149 A 349. (6) In re Kellner 121 E 243, 189 A 91 E & A. Abuse of discretion was apparently defined as "clear error" in Morris County B. & L. Assn. v. 

Walters 123 E 548, 198 A 756. (9) But no appeal lies from the order for a rehearing unless there is abuse of discretion in making that order. In re Kellner 121 E 243, 189 A 

91 E & A. See Morris County B. & L. Assn. v. Walters 123 E 548, 198 A 756. 

8464. Procedure on Appeal from Orphans' Court or Surrogate to Prerogative Court. (20) Additional time to file will be granted on application. Terhune v. Pinkney 39 E 494. 

(23) R.S. 2:30-4 (C S 3884 §195). 

132

§465. Hearing on Appeal. . . . . Burden of proof is on appel lant (11). (1) Smith v. Smith 48 E 566, 25 A 11; In re Bender 122 E 192, 192 A 

718, aff'd 123 E 171, 196 A 677; In re Le Van 123 E 463, 198 A 278, aff'd 

125 E 92, 4 A2d 280; In re Durr's Estate 89 A 996. Where the court has no original jurisdiction, no additional proofs may be taken. §278 at n 10; §465 n 3. Additional testimony may be taken in the Prerogative Court where it has original jurisdiction of the class of cases if probate or administration had been granted by it. Statutes frequently do not permit it to exercise its jurisdiction of a particular case because probate or administration was granted by the Surrogate or Orphans' Court; but this will not prevent the taking of testimony on appeal. See §450 at n 29; see In re Whitehead's Estate 

85 E 114, 121, 94 A 796, aff'd on op. in this respect 86 E 439, 99 A 1071. (2a) Note 2a annotates the first sentence of the second paragraph of the text. See Shotwell v. Shotwell 85 E 101, 95 A 365, aff'd on op. 85 E 

594, 96 A 398; Bioren v. Nesler 76 E 576, 74 A 791. 

See §278 n 1. 

(6) 

Contra In re Bender 122 E 192, 192 A 718, aff'd 123 E 171, 196

A 677 (this case seems sound. There is no reason why new issues may not be raised in an appellate court in an accounting as in any proceeding). 

Strike out the words repeated in the text "exceptions to the allowance of commissions and". (7) (9) A fortiori is this so in the Court of Errors. In re Bryan 125 E 

471, 5 A2d 774; In re Schimmel 125 E 88, 4 A2d 382 E & A (but despite this ruling the reviewing court must fully examine and analyze the evidence to determine whether the conclusions are consistent therewith). (11) In re Brugnoli's Estate 97 E 349, 127 A 165 E & A. Query when the matter is heard de novo. See In re Bottier's Estate 106 E 226, 150 A 

786 (where the Prerogative Court was considering the burden of proof on an appeal to the Orphans' Court heard de novo). 

§466. Dismissal of Appeal to Prerogative Court. . . . . An appeal may be dismissed if appellant fails to appear on the hearing (3a). (3a) In re Harrison 89 E 539, 105 A 238. (8) And so may an Orphans' Court. In re Koehler's Estate 102 E 

133, 140 A 15; §458 n 18. But an appeal will not be reinstated when the appellant could have avoided the dismissal by an application to extend time. 

Terhune v. Pinkney 39 E 494. 

$467. Proceedings in Prerogative and Orphans' Courts Subsequent to Appeal. 

(1) As in an appellate court of equity, the Prerogative Court where it remands the case, directs the lower court to enter such decree as it deems should be entered. In re Queen's Estate 82 E 583, 89 A 290.

one years, and at least be adopted; years older than the child herein sought to that they desire to adopt a minor child aged prior to who resides with them, at the address aforesaid, and who has been in their custody and care for a period, continuously, of more than the date of their said petition: that the parents of said child are that the of said child ha given consent to such adoption which consent is in writing, duly acknowledged as required by the ADOPTION ACTS, and which was presented with said petition; (If the child is over fourteen, h consent is also necessary) that said child has no property whatsoever, except that said petitioners prayed that the court would entertain a proceeding for the adoption of said child by your petitioners, as prescribed by law, and would inquire into the merits of their said petition, and after proceedings had that a Decree may be granted, filed and recorded in accordance with such law, granting permission to them to adopt said child, and authorizing and approving said adoption, and that the Court would change the name of 

Isaid child to

Whereupon, and upon reading the said petition, affidavit and consent the Court Ordered that the same be filed with the Clerk of this Court, and the same were filed accordingly, and the court did by its order dated the day of in accordance with the provisions of the ADOPTION ACT did order an investigation for the purpose of verifying the allegations set forth in the petition, and did appoint 19 the day of 19 for the hearing of said petition and report and the examination of the parties in interest: and

Whereas, upon the day so appointed the court did proceed to a full hearing of said petition and report and the examination of the petitioners and of all parties in interest, under oath, and the court from the testimony and from the investigation caused to be made, is of the opinion that the facts stated in said petition are true, and upon the said examination is satisfied that the petitioners are of good moral character and of reputable standing in the community and of ability to properly maintain and educate the said child sought to be adopted, reference being had to the condition in life of said child's parent, and furthermore, that the best interests of the said child will be promoted by such adoption:

NOW, THEREFORE, the court doth, by virtue of the act of the Legislature entitled, "An Act concerning minors, their adoption, custody and maintenance, and the descent, distribution and inheritance of their property and the property of their natural and adopting parents and amending sections 

9:3-2, 9:3-3, 9:3-4, 9:3-5, 9:3-6, 9:3-8 and 9:3-11 of the Revised Statutes." 

Approved June 14th, 1938, effective January 1st, 1939, and the supplements and amends thereto, Order, Adjudge and Decree that the prayer of the said petitioners be and the same is hereby granted, and that said minor child as aforesaid, shall hereafter be known by the name of and the court doth hereby declare and adjudge that from the date of this decree the 147

-  

rights, duties, privileges and relations heretofore existing between the said and parents shall be and are in all respects at an end, excepting the right of inheritance; and further, that the rights, duties, privileges and relations between the said child and the said and parents by adoption, are and shall henceforth in all respects be the same, including the right of inheritance and succession to personal property, as if the said child had been born to his said adopted parents in lawful wedlock, except only as otherwise provided in the said act of the Legislature above mentioned, and the several supplements and amendments thereto. 

It is further Ordered that this Decree and all proceedings herein be recorded except as by the statute otherwise provided.

1. Your petitioner was, by this Honorable Court, on duly appointed guardian of the said 19 a mental incompetent, and thereafter Letters of Guardianship were issued to your petitioner, and it has undertaken, and now is performing, its duties as such guardian. 

2. Among the personal estate of the said which has come into the hands of your petitioner, are certain securities hereinafter set forth, which for certain reasons your petitioner believes should be sold, and the proceeds derived therefrom invested in securities that are recognized by law as legal investments for trustees. 

3. The said is ninety years of age and your petitioner has been informed by competent medical advisers, and believes, that she never will be restored to sound reason, and yet she may live for a period of some years in her present condition. 

4. The names and amounts of such securities held, together with the reasons causing your petitioner to believe that the same should be sold, are as follows: (List securities) 

5. The said in addition to her investments, has also liquid funds deposited in banks which is immediately available for her use at any time. These funds, together with the income from her invested estate, appear to be ample to maintain and support her in comfort for several years and probably for the rest of her life. It, therefore, appears to be advisable to invest in income producing securities the proceeds of a sale of the securities set forth above.

Your petitioner therefore prays that this Honorable Court will approve the sale of the aforesaid securities by your petitioner at about the prices mentioned above and the investment of the proceeds therefrom in such securities as are recognized by law as legal investments for trustees.

Note 1: Law. Relating to minor's estate: Dispensing with Guardian ship. Value of Real Estate not exceeding $100. Value of Personal Estate not exceeding $500. P. L. of New Jersey 1938, Chapter 37, amending Section 3:7-29 of the Revised Statutes. 

1. Section 3:7-29 of the Revised Statutes is hereby amended to read as follows: 

3:7-29. In all cases where the value of the real estate of any minor, shall not exceed one hundred dollars and the value of the personal estate of any minor shall not exceed five hundred dollars, either parent of such minor or a person standing in loco parentis to such minor, shall be entitled to receive the same for the use and benefit of such minor without being appointed guardian, and any bank, building and loan association, or other corporation or any person or society who shall have possession of any assets of such minor and shall pay or deliver the same to his parent or person standing in loco parentis, upon the making and execution of an affidavit before the ordinary, orphans' court or surrogate, setting up that affiant is the parent of such a minor or a person standing in loco parentis to such minor and that the value of such minor's real estate will not exceed one hundred dollars and that the said minor's personal estate shall not exceed five hundred dollars, and specifying in detail said assets shall be forever discharged from all claims by any guardian who may thereafter be appointed or by any other person, for the assets so paid or delivered. 1 2. All acts or parts of acts inconsistent herewith are hereby repealed. 

3. This act shall take effect immediately. 

Approved March 28, 1938. 

Note 2: Required as a matter of good practice. Consent is required of all minors over fourteen years of age where both parents of minor are dead and an affidavit is executed by a person standing in loco parentis. Where the minor is over or under the age of fourteen years, consent is required of the parent (not the affiant) or parents or person standing in loco parentis to said minor, in favor of affiant. 166

not proper, and that, in loaning the moneys of the said estate upon such improper and insufficient security, the said administratrix as afore said, has despoiled the estate entrusted to her as such administratrix.

(Description of property.) 

(Details of any change of assets.)

Note 1: Always show balance from prior account, if any.

Note 2: It is the duty of executors, administrators, guardians and trustees to keep accounts, and to take and preserve vouchers. It is not possible for the surrogate to intelligently audit an account from the mere production of checks; it is his duty to inspect the items for which allowance is prayed, and, if there be any which appear to be unjustified by law, to report the fact to the Orphans' Court,-so preserve and submit the proper vouchers. 

Note 3: Inasmuch as the purpose of an accounting is to show the balance with which accountant should be charged, an accountant who has made investment of funds of the estate should not pray allowance for the moneys so disbursed, nor should he, on the other hand, in the event of the payment to him of moneys secured by investments which he holds, charge himself with the amount of moneys so paid to him. These matters should be shown by the schedule of assets required to be annexed to the account,—as only the form in which the balance is held has been changed.

MODEL FORM OF WILL EXHIBIT WILL OF.. 

IN THE NAME OF GOD, AMEN. 

of the City of in the County of and State of being of sound mind, memory and understanding, do make and publish this my last Will and Testament, in manner following (hereby revoking all other Wills by me heretofore made), that is to say:

FIRST: It is my will, and I do order, that all my just debts and funeral and testamentary expenses be duly paid and satisfied after my decease. 

SECOND: I give, devise and bequeath to my two sons, and absolutely and in equal shares (subject, however, to the charges thereon hereinafter mentioned), all the right, title and interest which I shall have, at the time of my death, in and to all the assets of the business of the manufacture, sale and repair of carriages, wagons, sleighs and other vehicles; which said business is now conducted by me, in aforesaid, under the name and style of

It being intended to include in the bequest and devise in this section of my Will contained, all the right, title and interest which I shall then have in and to the following (as well as any other assets then pertaining to the said business, excepting the factory premises hereinafter mentioned) to wit: The stock of goods, whether finished or unfinished then on hand, the stock then on hand, of materials for manufacture, 266

FORMS 

book accounts, and bills receivable; also including the following, which shall have been taken in the course of such business, to wit, any and all promissory notes, checks, drafts, bills of exchange, bonds and evidences of debt whatsoever, and money; also including all my right, title and interest in and to all such assets of said business as shall then be in the form of mortgages, whether upon real estate or chattels. It is not my intent, however, to include in the bequest and devise in this Second Section of my Will, contained, either any mortgage or other evidence of debt or property, real or personal, whatsoever, not taken or acquired in the course of such business, and not forming strictly and distinctly a part of the assets of such business at the time of my death. Nor is it my intent to include in the property or assets in this said Second Section, devised and bequeathed the factory premises whereon said business is now carried on (and situate in the City of aforesaid), nor any interest therein or in any part thereof. 

THIRD: All the assets and property in the foregoing Second Section of this my Last Will and Testament devised and bequeathed I hereby charge with the payment of all the debts which shall be owing by, in or concerning the said business at the time of my death, whether the said business shall be then conducted by me alone, or by a firm or partnership with which I shall then be connected. And I hereby further charge all the said assets and property in the said Second Section devised and bequeathed with the payment to my beloved wife, of the sum of dollars per month for and during her natural life. FOURTH: I give and bequeath absolutely to my said wife all my household furniture, books, pictures and other works of art, diamonds, jewelry, trinkets, china, crockery and table ware; as well as all goods and chattels whatsoever of household use or for household ornament, and which shall form part of my estate at the time of my death. And I also bequeath absolutely to my said wife all my conveyances which, at the time of my death, shall not have been used solely and exclusively for the purpose of my said business. 

FIFTH: All the residue of my estate, real and personal, whatsoever and wheresoever, I give, devise and bequeath to my executors, hereinafter named, in trust, nevertheless, to keep the same safely invested, with power to retain any investment made by me in my lifetime, and to pay the net interest and income thereof, at frequent and convenient intervals, not less often than quarterly, to my said wife, for and during her natural life.

SIXTH: Each of the provisions hereinbefore made for my said wife is in lieu of dower in any of my real estate. 

SEVENTH: Upon the death of my said wife, I give, devise and bequeath the principal or corpus of the said residue of my interest, absolutely and in fee simple, to my said two sons, to be divided between them equally, share and share alike. and 

And in case either of my said sons shall predecease me, and leave a widow and lawful child or children, then I give, devise and bequeath the equal one-half part of the said principal or corpus of the said residue, absolutely and in fee simple, as follows: one-third of such one-half part to such widow and the remaining two-thirds of such one-half part to such child (or children as the case may be) of my son so dying. But if my son so dying shall not leave a widow but shall leave lawful child or children, then I give, 267

devise and bequeath, the whole of such one-half part of the said principal or corpus of the said residue, absolutely and in fee simple to such child (or children as the case may be) of my son so dying. But if my son so dying shall leave no lawful child, but shall leave a widow then I give, devise and bequeath the whole of such one-half part of the said principal or corpus of the said residue,absolutely and in fee simple to such widow. 

But if either of my said sons shall predecease me, and shall leave neither a widow nor a lawful child, and my other son shall survive me, then I give devise and bequeath absolutely and in fee simple to my son so surviving me the equal one-half part of the said principal of the said residue which would have gone to such deceased son had he survived me; that is to say: in such case I give, devise and bequeath the whole of the said residuary principal or corpus to such son surviving me. 

EIGHTH: I appoint my wife, and my said sons, and the Executors of this my Last Will and Testament, and Trustees thereunder, without bond, and authorize and empower them to sell and convey, in their discretion, all or any part of the real estate whereof I may die seized. 

IN WITNESS WHEREOF I have hereunto set my hand and seal this day of in the year of our Lord One Thousand Nine Hundred and Attest: 

SECTION OF WILL CREATING TRUST 

"NINTH: I give and devise to my said Executors, in trust for the purposes hereinafter, in this section of my will, mentioned, all the lands and premises hereinafter in this said section mentioned, and situate in the said 

City of to wit all the lands and premises, with the factories and other buildings thereon, wherein and whereon (in partnership with my said ), I am now carrying on business under the firm name of son also all my right, title and interest in all that tract of land, and in the brick building thereon, known as number

Street, upon which last mentioned land and premises (in partnership with my said son ), I am now carrying on the business, under the firm name of ; in trust, nevertheless, for my said executors to hold the same for and during the natural life of my said wife with power, however, to my said executors, in their discretion, to sell and convey all or any part thereof, (but provided that the aforesaid factory lands and premises shall not be sold for less than dollars) and the proceeds of such sale or sales, to invest in safe and approved securities; with power also to change such securities, from time to time, as my said Executors, in their discretion, shall see fit; (with power, also, to my said executors, in their discretion, in the lifetime of my said wife, to obtain from the lessor of the said tract Number Street, a new lease thereof to take effect upon the expiration of the term of the present lease thereof); and in trust, further for my said executors to collect, the rents, issues, profits, interest and income arising from the lands and premises in this section mentioned, and arising from such proceeds and securities, and to pay thereout the taxes and assessments imposes, from time to time, upon such part of said lands and premises remaining unsold, and the cost of keeping the buildings thereon safely insured and in repair; and 268

FORMS 

to pay also the ground rent falling due from time to time upon said land Number Street, (which interest therein shall remain unsold), and to pay also the interest accruing, from time to time, upon any mortgage or mortgages comprising said lands and premises, or any part thereof, and made in my lifetime; then out of the said rents, issues, profits, interest and income thereafter remaining, to pay to my said wife dollars per annum in equal monthly payments, for and during her natural life; and in trust further, out of the residue of the aforesaid rents, issues, profits, interest and income from time to time remaining after the payments hereinabove, in this section directed, to reduce the principal of such mortgage or mortgages as rapidly as possible, until the same shall be satisfied. Upon the death of my said wife, I give, devise and bequeath to my four children absolutely, and in equal shares, the whole of the corpus of the lands and premises in this section of my will mentioned, and then so held by my said executors in trust, and also all the principal or corpus of any and all such proceeds of the lands and premises so sold and of all the securities or other property whatsoever in which any part of such proceeds shall be then invested, together with any and all income then accumulated upon any or all the principal, or corpus in this section of my will mentioned."

SIMPLE FORM OF CODICIL TO A WILL Preamble: 

"Whereas, in any of the ninth section of my said last will and testament I have directed the executors thereof and trustees thereunder to pay to my wife, out of certain net rents, issues, profits, interest and income therein mentioned, dollars per annum, in equal monthly payments, for and during her natural life; now I direct the said executors and trustees to pay to my said wife out of such net rents, issues, profits, interest and income and instead of such sum of dollars per annum, the sum of dollars per annum, in equal monthly payments, for and during her natural life."

Note 1: Insert Republication Clause and Testimonium Clause. 

Note 2: Insert Attestation Clause.
